  - **Fix.** `src/lib/sentry-scrub.ts` exports a `beforeSend` and a `beforeBreadcrumb`, and both `Sentry.init` calls (browser, server and edge) use them. `beforeSend` cuts the page address at its `?` or `#`, drops the request's query string, body and cookies and the cookie and authorization headers, cuts the referer the same way, cuts an error message of the API client's shape (`Failed to …: <status> <response body>`) after the status, and drops a `?name=…` or `#name=…` from any address inside a message, with or without a host. `beforeBreadcrumb` drops console output, cuts the query off request and navigation addresses, and cuts a click's element path at its first attribute: `BookCard` has `title={book.title}` and `ChapterTab` has `aria-label="Open chapter …"`, and the SDK records both.
  - **Already done.** The issue's first criterion, the build arguments, was met by #779 after the issue was written: both images have the address in `.next/static`. The CSP entry was there since #334; an image built with an address allows it with no setting at run time.
  - **Not covered.** An error message of any other shape is sent as it is. The click breadcrumb still carries the element's id and classes. Staging's image is built without an address (its GitHub environment has no `NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_DSN` variable), so the SDK stays off there until the operator sets one (#992). The Privacy Policy's Sentry row is unchanged: an error message can still quote content.

- **The settings object does not print its secrets (#930, P1.26.1)**:
  - **Problem.** `Settings` is a pydantic model, and a model's repr prints every field value. Anything that rendered `settings` printed the live credentials: a log line or f-string naming it, and pytest explaining a failed assertion that involved it. Measured on main with a made-up value in each of the thirteen settings the issue names: `repr(settings)`, a log line and `pytest -vv` on a failing test each printed all thirteen.
  - **Fix.** Sixteen fields are declared with `Field(..., repr=False)`: the thirteen, `AI_BASE_URL` and `BETTER_AUTH_URL`, which match the same naming rule, and `E2E_EXEMPT_EMAILS`, which holds addresses. Each stays a `str`, so no caller changes. On the branch the same three print none.
  - **Guard.** `tests/test_core/test_settings_repr.py` takes every setting whose name has `KEY`, `SECRET`, `PASSWORD`, `DSN`, `TOKEN`, `URI`, `URL` or `EMAIL(S)` as a whole word and fails if it is not hidden, so a new credential cannot be added without it. `AI_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS_*` is not matched: a token budget is not a token.
  - **Measured differently from the issue.** The issue says an `AttributeError` on a missing setting embeds the repr in its message. With the installed pydantic the message names only the attribute; the values came from pytest rendering the object. The test the issue asks for is there and passed before the change.
  - **Not covered.** A value read out of the settings and printed on its own (`f"{settings.OPENAI_API_KEY}"`) is printed. `settings.model_dump()` still returns everything. The guard goes by field name: a credential under a name with none of those words, or behind an alias, is not caught.

- **A backend error report no longer carries what the user wrote, and one failure is one report (#808, P1.26)**:
  - **Problem.** Sentry ran on the SDK's defaults. A probe of main found, in the events for one failed request: a frame local holding manuscript text, the request body, the session cookie's value, an email address from an INFO log line, the query string, and the colliding email quoted in MongoDB's duplicate-key message. A prompt sent to the model was in the frame locals of the model client's own error report. One 500 produced two events. The container has no git, so events had no release.
  - **No log line reaches Sentry.** Every ERROR log line was an event and every INFO line a breadcrumb. A log message is free text that a key-based scrubber cannot read, and 133 calls in the backend log at ERROR; review found one endpoint (question generation) that could send seven events for one failure, several quoting the question it was saving. The logging integration now sends nothing. The one report for a 500 is the global handler's `capture_exception`, which is what #334 set out to do.
  - **Options.** `include_local_variables=False`, `max_request_body_size="never"`, the SDK's key-based scrubber extended with the field names that hold user text or identity (`content`, `title`, `email` and others), and a `before_send` that drops the query string and the referer and cuts a duplicate-key message before the value.
  - **422s.** Both validation handlers log a warning that names the fields and the kind of error. Neither logs a value or a traceback.
  - **Release.** The image build passes the commit as `GIT_SHA`; the backend image keeps it in its environment and Sentry is started with it.
  - **Measured, not changed.** MongoDB commands made through a client created after the SDK starts are recorded as breadcrumbs with every value already replaced by the SDK; a test with a real database pins that. **Corrected in #812:** the app's own database client is created at import, before the SDK starts, and the SDK does not watch it, so in the running app no database command is recorded at all.
  - **What this costs.** A failure the code handles and logs at ERROR is no longer reported through its log line. **Corrected in #812:** this entry first said such a failure is not reported at all. Measured since: a route that raises an `HTTPException` with a 5xx status is reported by the SDK's Starlette integration, as an `HTTPException` carrying the detail text and not the original error. What goes unreported is a failure answered below 500, or logged and carried on from, or in a background task: #996. A failed model call is also reported by the SDK's own model-client integration (#994).
  - **Not covered.** The text of an exception other than a duplicate key is sent as it is; a message that quotes user input still reaches Sentry. Nothing was sent to a real Sentry project: staging has no DSN (#992). The Privacy Policy's Sentry row is unchanged for that reason and because the frontend's Sentry is #809.

- **In production /health fails when the box cannot store uploads, bill or report errors, and liveness has its own route (#807, P1.25)**:
  - **Problem.** `/health` checked MongoDB and two secrets. A production deployment with no storage provider, no Stripe values or no `SENTRY_DSN` passed the deploy gate. The container's liveness check used the same route, so a database outage marked a live process unhealthy.
  - **Checks.** `/health` now also reports `storage`, `billing` and `error_reporting`: `ok`, `disabled`, or `missing:` with the names of the unset settings, never their values. Storage asks `settings.storage_provider` (#806); billing needs `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY`, `STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET` and `STRIPE_PRICE_ID_PRO`; error reporting needs `SENTRY_DSN`.
  - **Fatal in production only.** With `ENVIRONMENT=production` any `missing:` is a 503. Elsewhere the same lines are reported and the answer stays 200, so staging keeps passing its gate and shows from outside what it has configured. The decision reads the validated `ENVIRONMENT` setting only; `NODE_ENV`, which the other production guards also honour, does not count here, because one left in a staging box's shared `.env` would fail its deploy for having no Stripe.
  - **`BILLING_ENABLED`.** New setting, default true. False reports billing as `disabled` for a deployment that sells nothing on purpose. It changes only what `/health` reports.
  - **`/health/live`.** Answers `{"status": "alive"}` and touches nothing. `docker-compose.yml`'s backend health check uses it; `/health` stays the readiness probe and the deploy gate.
  - **Not covered.** For storage the production 503 is behind another guard: since #806 a production backend without a provider does not start at all. The checks are presence checks; a wrong Stripe key or a dead DSN passes. The frontend service waits for the backend's container health, which is now liveness, so it can start while the database is still away.

- **Where uploads go is decided in one place, and production refuses local disk (#806, P1.24)**:
  - **Problem.** The storage provider was chosen three ways. The `AWS_*` and `CLOUDINARY_*` fields on `Settings` were declared and never read. `get_cloud_storage_service` read `os.getenv` and needed every key of a provider. The static mount in `main.py` looked at one key of each. With half a Cloudinary configuration the service wrote uploads to local disk and the mount, seeing "cloud", never served them.
  - **One answer.** `settings.storage_provider` returns `cloudinary`, `s3` or `local`; a provider counts only with all of its values set, and `settings.storage_fields_missing` names the absent half. The service and `mount_local_uploads` both ask it.
  - **The mount and the first upload.** The mount was also skipped when `uploads/` did not exist at startup, and the first upload is what creates it, so on a fresh checkout nothing uploaded was served until the next restart. It is mounted whenever the provider is local. The demo found this: the test for the mount had created the directory first.
  - **Production.** `validate_production_security` refuses to start with `ENVIRONMENT=production` and no complete provider: "no upload storage is configured, so uploads would go to local disk in production", with the settings to set.
  - **Other deployed environments.** In the container image `/app` belongs to root, so `uploads/` cannot be created. The backend starts all the same, because the upload service is loaded on first use; the first upload then failed with `PermissionError: 'uploads'`. It fails with a message now: no cloud storage is configured, the directory cannot be created, what to set.
  - **Changed for developers.** The provider now follows `backend/.env`, which `Settings` reads, where before only exported variables counted. A checkout that wrote to local disk and has a complete provider in `.env` moves to that provider; its old `/uploads/...` files are then no longer served, and startup logs a warning saying so. The test session empties the six values first, so a `.env` that names a real bucket cannot send test uploads to it.
  - **Whitespace.** The six storage values are stripped, like the Stripe ones: a quoted space or a trailing newline would otherwise count as a configured provider and get past the production check.
  - **A guard in the frontend tests.** `subprocessors.test.ts` sweeps the backend's reads of the process environment and was anchored on the Cloudinary read this change removed. It failed in CI, not locally: the commit hooks run frontend tests only for frontend paths. It is anchored on a read that still exists.
  - **Not covered.** Whether staging has a provider configured is still not known: nothing public shows it and its `.env` was not read. `/health` does not report storage (#807). No upload to a real provider was made, and nothing checks that configured credentials work. A failed upload is still a 500 for the client.

- **A refused AI request shows what happened and what to do, not a status and JSON (#803, P1.21)**:
  - **Problem.** Five client methods (`transformChapterStyle`, `enhanceChapterText`, `enhanceVoiceTranscription`, `generateChapterQuestions`, `regenerateChapterQuestions`) threw the status and the raw response body as their message, and the four screens that call them printed it. The status never reached the classifier, so a plan refusal never showed the Upgrade notification and an exhausted allowance looked like any failure.
  - **Client.** The five go through `aiError()`, like the other AI methods. It now also carries the error code, the time to retry (body or `Retry-After`), and the allowance period from `X-AI-Quota-Period`, and turns a 422 for a text over its cap into a sentence with the limit.
  - **Classifier.** `handleAIServiceError` treats a 429 with an allowance period as a quota: not retryable, titled "Daily AI limit reached" or "Monthly AI limit reached". The message prefix it used to recognise a quota by is kept as the fallback. A provider rate limit says how many seconds to wait, and a request the backend refused shows what the backend said about it.
  - **Screens.** `StyleTransformer`, `ContentEnhancer`, `VoiceEnhancer` and `QuestionContainer` (generate and regenerate all) hand the error to the shared handler, which announces it once, and show its message. Their own error toasts are gone.
  - **Backend.** The page and the API are on different origins, and the CORS setup named no response headers a script may read, so the page could not read `X-AI-Quota-Period` or `Retry-After` whatever the client did. `Access-Control-Expose-Headers` now lists them with `X-AI-Quota-Limit`.
  - **Not covered.** The question panel still labels its button "Retry" after a refusal that retrying cannot change. The notification for a rate limit has no retry action of its own; the dialog's button sends the request again. `regenerateQuestion` (one question) and the other non-AI client methods keep their own error handling. The Upgrade button was not followed in the demo.

- **An error tells the client a fixed message; what the provider or the database said stays in the log (#802, P1.20)**:
  - **Problem.** Two roads carried upstream error text to clients. Transform, enhance and transcription cleanup caught every AI failure and returned `str(e)`, which the routes wrapped in a 503: a provider's rate limit lost its status and its retry time, and a rejected request arrived with the provider's own body, organisation id and request id included. And the shared handlers in `backend/app/utils/error_handlers.py` put `str(error)` into the response, so a database driver's error reached the client with its collection and index names.
  - **AI failures.** The three rewrite flows re-raise `AIServiceError`, as drafts have since #352. One function, `ai_error_http_exception`, turns it into the response on every AI route: 429 for a rate limit, 503 for an outage, 400 for a rejected request, 500 otherwise, with `Retry-After` whenever the error knows when to come back. It replaces four copies of the same forty-line `except` chain in `books.py`, which did not set the header. The message is the service's fixed text: `AIInvalidRequestError` and the unexpected-error case no longer build theirs from the provider's.
  - **Shared handlers.** `handle_generic_error`, `handle_question_generation_error`, `handle_response_save_error` and `handle_rating_save_error` answer with a fixed message and the request id. The exception text is in the log line each already wrote, next to the same id.
  - **Left as it was.** `analyze-summary` still answers any AI failure with its own 503 (`AI_ANALYSIS_FAILED`, fixed message): the contents wizard treats a failed analysis as no analysis and carries on.
  - **Changed for API callers.** On the three rewrite routes a rate limit is a 429 and a rejected request a 400, where both were 503; their error `detail` is the structured object the other AI routes use, not a string. Every AI error detail now carries the same six fields.
  - **Not covered.** Routes that pass on the message of an error our own code raised (`detail=str(e)` for a `ValueError` or an export error, in `export.py`, `chapters.py` and `books.py`) are unchanged; those messages are written for the user, but nothing stops a library's `ValueError` from taking the same road. The frontend still shows these responses as raw status and body (#803).

- **Exports number chapters by their place in the outline (#805, P1.23)**:
  - **Problem.** PDF, Word, EPUB and Markdown numbered the flattened list of chapters, so a book with two chapters and two subchapters under the first came out as Chapter 1 to Chapter 4, in the body and in each table of contents. In Word all four were top-level headings and the EPUB navigation was one flat list.
  - **Numbering.** `_flatten_chapters` in `backend/app/services/export_service.py` gives each chapter its outline number while it flattens: 1, 1.1, 1.2, 2. A chapter is headed "Chapter 1: Title" and anything under one "1.1 Title"; a contents row reads "1. Title" or "1.1 Title". The four builders take both from `_chapter_heading` and `_contents_entry`.
  - **Levels.** Word uses a heading one level down for each level of nesting, the PDF a smaller title style, EPUB `<h2>` and below, and the EPUB navigation nests subchapters under their chapter.
  - **What is counted.** Numbers count what is exported, so they have no gaps. Empty chapters are still left out by default, with one change: a chapter with no text of its own is kept when a chapter under it is kept, because its subchapters need its heading. Such a heading gets no "(No content yet)" line and no page of its own; in the EPUB it gets no document of its own, since a reader starts a new page for each one: the heading opens the first document under it, and the navigation entry points there. On main it was dropped and its subchapters were numbered as chapters.
  - **Not covered.** Each subchapter still starts on a new page in PDF and Word, as every entry did before. The PDF contents page has no page numbers, as before. `export_service.py` is over the 500-line limit and this adds to it. No ereader or word processor was opened: the files were read back with pypdf, python-docx and the EPUB's own XHTML.

- **A rewrite that lost most of the text is refused, and the preview shows the word counts (#983, P1.19.1)**:
  - **Problem.** Transform, enhance and dictation cleanup refused an answer the model reported as cut off, and nothing else. A model that condenses a long text and reports a normal finish passed, and the page offered the result as the finished rewrite. In live runs for #801 a 5,000-word chapter came back at 28% to 49% of its length, as a 200.
  - **Refusal.** `shortened_rewrite_error` in `backend/app/services/ai_service.py` compares the two texts by the characters a reader sees: tags and whitespace left out, entities decoded (a chapter pasted from rich text is full of `&nbsp;`, and a rewrite that says the same with ordinary spaces has lost nothing). Words were the first measure and the wrong one: Chinese, Japanese and Thai are written without spaces, and the same text marked up or spaced differently counted as shorter. Transform and enhance refuse a result under 60% of the source's length; cleanup, which removes filler, under 40%. A source under 1,000 characters is not checked. The route answers 413 with `error_code: REWRITE_SHORTENED` and a message carrying the percentage, through the same helper as `REWRITE_TRUNCATED`. The provider was called, so the generation stays charged.
  - **Why those shares.** Against gpt-oss:120b a faithful transform kept 94% to 112% of the words and a condensed one 28% to 54%; cleaning up a dictation that was a quarter filler kept 58% to 69%.
  - **Preview.** `RewriteWordCounts` puts one line under the before and after panes of all three dialogs: "2,868 words before, 2,320 after (19% shorter)". The response already carried counts, taken by splitting the raw HTML, where two paragraphs side by side run together into one word; now that they are shown they are counted from the visible text.
  - **Not covered.** A rewrite between the threshold and the source's length is returned, and it is the author who has to notice the line. Length is a crude measure: a rewrite of the right length that dropped a section and padded another passes. The shares come from one model. The dialogs show the 413 as raw status and JSON, like every error from these client methods (#803).

- **Transform, enhance and transcription cleanup work on a chapter of ordinary length (#801, P1.19)**:
  - **Problem.** The three rewrite flows ran on the default model class (`AI_MODEL_DEFAULT`, gpt-4: an 8,192-token context shared by the prompt and the answer) with a fixed 4,000-token completion budget. A chapter over about 3,000 words could not come back whole.
  - **Model and budget.** They now run on the long-output class (`AI_MODEL_LONG_OUTPUT`, gpt-4o by default), as drafts and contents already do. The completion budget follows the text: a third of its characters, never under the 4,000 these flows always had, clamped to `AI_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS_LONG` (8,000) by the request method. `metadata.model_used` names the long-output model.
  - **Cap.** `MAX_AI_CONTENT_CHARS` moves from 20,000 to 32,000 characters. Measured with the gpt-4o tokenizer, 5,000 words of English prose with its punctuation, as the editor stores it, is about 31,000 characters and 6,200 tokens (4.9 characters a token; technical text is nearer 4.4), so at 32,000 a rewrite of prose can run a fifth longer than its source inside the budget. A first measurement on words alone gave 5.6 characters a token and a cap of 35,000; the reviewer caught it.
  - **A cut-off rewrite is a 413.** When the model stops at its budget the route answers 413 with `error_code: REWRITE_TRUNCATED`. It answered 503, which invites a retry that ends at the same place. Other failures of these routes are still 503.
  - **Regression fix for #800.** #800 cut a chapter's stored title and description to length with a slice, and a stored `null` (which no write path rejects) raised `TypeError`: a draft for such a chapter failed with a 503, and question generation with a 500. Found by the review bot after #800 merged. Both read the value through `str(value or "")` now, and so does the stored chapter content the question prompt uses, which crashed on a number (older than #800, found in this review).
  - **Not covered.** A model can return a much shorter text and report a normal finish; in live runs against gpt-oss:120b a 5,000-word chapter came back at 28% to 49% of its length, every time, as a success (#983). The numbers assume English prose: technical text near the cap has almost no room to grow, and a script that takes a token or more per character reaches the budget far sooner; both get the 413 after a charged call. Nothing here was run against gpt-4o itself, and a full 8,000-token answer has not been timed against the 120-second request budget (#799).

- **AI requests are capped in size, and an oversize one is refused before it costs anything (#800, P1.18)**:
  - **Problem.** The AI quota counts calls, not tokens, and the routes that send text to the model read a free-form `dict` of any size. On main a 400,000-character chapter and 101 answers of 10,000 characters each both went to the provider whole, each for one generation.
  - **Caps.** `backend/app/schemas/ai_input.py` holds the request models and the numbers: text to transform, enhance or clean up is at most 20,000 characters (those calls allow 4,000 completion tokens, so a longer text could not come back whole); a list of answers is at most 100 pairs (what one batch save takes), 1,000 characters a question, 10,000 an answer and 100,000 in total; a writing style is at most 100. `generate-toc`, `generate-draft`, `transform-style`, `enhance-text` and `enhance-transcription` take those models. Over a cap is a 422 with no model call, and the quota's charge is refunded (#798).
  - **Saved answers.** An answer comes back later as model input, so the three routes that save one have the same cap: the contents wizard's `PUT question-responses`, a chapter answer, and the batch save.
  - **Stored text.** A request body is not the only road to a prompt (found in review). A chapter's stored title and description went into the draft prompt whole, and the title into the question prompts, and `PUT /toc` never limited them; a prompt now uses the first 500 and 2,000 characters. `generate-toc` falls back to answers saved on the book, which may predate the caps; they are checked like a request body and refused with a 422. A generated clarifying question is cut to 1,000 characters, since the wizard sends it back with its answer. Behind all of it, `_make_openai_request` refuses any prompt over 150,000 characters before the call is attempted.
  - **The refusal.** `validation_exception_handler` returned each error's `input` and logged the exception with its traceback, which contains the input too, so a refused 400,000-character request was sent back and written to the log. Both are gone. The handler also answered 500 for any custom validator's error, because the error holds the exception object; it is encoded now.
  - **Changed for API callers.** A malformed answer list (not a list, an item that is not an object, a missing `question` or `answer`) is a 422 from the request model where the handlers used to answer 400. An explicit `null` for `question_responses` is a 422 as well; it used to mean "use the saved answers", which an empty list or no body still does.
  - **Not covered.** The pages do not check these limits before sending, and show the 422 as raw text. A chapter with more than 100 answered questions, or answers over 100,000 characters in total, cannot be drafted from the page until it sends fewer. The prompt ceiling surfaces as a server error, not a 4xx, because nothing known reaches it. Limits are in characters, so text in a script that takes more tokens per character costs more per call. The numbers are judgement, not measurement.

- **Exports work for any book title and are logged, and the download no longer races its own cleanup (#804, P1.22)**:
  - **Title in the header.** The export routes put the title into `Content-Disposition` as written. Starlette encodes headers as latin-1, so a CJK or Cyrillic title raised a 500 after the whole file had been built, in all four formats. `_download_headers` in `backend/app/api/endpoints/export.py` now sends an ASCII fallback in `filename` and the real name in `filename*` (RFC 6266), for PDF, Word, EPUB and Markdown.
  - **Access log.** Each export called `log_access(chapter_id=None)`, which failed validation because the model required a chapter, and the handler swallowed the error: no export was ever recorded. `chapter_id` is optional now, and an export writes one `export_<format>` row with no chapter.
  - **Blob URL.** The download helper revoked the blob URL in the same tick as the click. It now waits 60 seconds. The export page used its own copy of that code and now calls the helper.
  - **Download name.** The page names the file itself, with an ASCII-only filter that turned a CJK title into underscores (export page) or into no name at all (book page). Both now use one builder, `filenameStem`, which keeps letters and digits of any script and falls back to "book" when nothing is left; the export page's names change from underscores to hyphens.
  - **Not covered.** The account-level export (`/users/me/export`, #790) is still not logged. A real browser was not driven; the header and the names are covered by tests.

- **AI requests have a time budget, and a refused credential or billing refusal is treated as an outage (#799, P1.17)**:
  - **Timeout.** The OpenAI client was built with no timeout, so the SDK's 600 seconds applied. With three attempts, a stalled provider could hold a worker thread for half an hour on the executor that also runs uploads and payments. `AI_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` (default 120, per attempt) is now passed to the client.
  - **Rejected credential.** A 401 or 403 from the provider was retried and then reported as an unexpected error. It now raises `AIProviderAuthError` after one attempt: not retryable, logged CRITICAL, answered to the user as 503 with the code `AI_PROVIDER_AUTH_FAILED` and no mention of keys.
  - **Billing refusal.** `insufficient_quota` and a 402 were already non-retryable since #775; their log line moved from ERROR to CRITICAL.
  - **Not covered.** 120 seconds is a guess that leaves room for a long draft; it has not been measured against real generations. The model call still runs on the shared default executor.

- **Rate limits are keyed on the route, and the AI quota charges only requests that may have reached the model (#798, P1.16)**:
  - **Rate-limit key.** The limiter's bucket was named for the concrete URL, so every book id (and every chapter id on transform-style) was a fresh bucket: "10 exports an hour" was 10 per book. It is now named for the route as declared, `/books/{book_id}/export/pdf`, with the URL as the fallback when no route matched. The 11th export across two books in a window is refused.
  - **Quota.** `get_ai_usage_quota` charged before validation, ownership, the plan gate and the rate limiter ran, and a refusal past the cap pushed the counter higher still. It is now a dependency with `yield`: it charges up front and refunds when the request fails without a model call having been attempted (the quota's own refusal, a validation error, a missing or foreign book, a rate limit). `AIService` marks the request the moment it hands a call to the provider, and from then on the charge stays whatever the outcome, including a 4xx such as a truncated draft: the provider may have billed for it.
  - **Tests.** `backend/tests/test_api/test_ai_quota_charging.py` reads the real counters after each kind of request. The per-route quota test used a 404 to spend the allowance; a 404 is free now, so it spends the allowance directly. `tests/yield_dependencies.py` lets unit tests call the dependency as a finished request.
  - **Not covered.** Each export format is its own route and so its own bucket: ten Markdown and ten PDF exports are both allowed in an hour. A handler that returns an error response without raising keeps its charge, as does a request that fails after a successful model call for an unrelated reason.

- **A stale or revoked session is handled instead of failing in place (#796, P1.14)**:
  - **Session too old to list devices.** better-auth lists sessions only for a session under a day old and answers 403 `SESSION_NOT_FRESH` after that. Settings showed a load error with a Retry button that could never work. It now says "You signed in more than a day ago. Sign in again to see your devices." with a link that comes back to Settings.
  - **"Sign out other devices" is always offered.** It was shown only when the list had loaded and contained another session, so it was missing exactly when the list was unavailable. It works on any valid session.
  - **One handler for a 401 from the backend.** Every backend call (`bookClient`, `useAuthFetch`, the avatar upload) goes through `apiFetch` in `frontend/src/lib/api/unauthorized.ts`. A 401 signs out, which clears the dead cookie, and sends the user to sign in with the current page as the place to return to. It runs once however many requests were in flight, and the caller still receives the response. Before this a revoked device kept looking signed in, because the frontend middleware only checks that a cookie exists, and every request failed.
  - **A failed sign-out is reported.** The account menu navigated home whether or not the sign-out worked, and the home page's button ignored the result. Both use `signOutOrNotify`, which shows "Could not sign out" and returns false; the menu goes home only on success.
  - **Tests.** Two specs in the real-auth Playwright suite (#843) run these against a real server: one ages a session by two days and checks the prompt and that the other device is signed out; one revokes a session and checks the browser lands on sign-in with its cookie gone.
  - **Not covered.** The server still swallows a failed `deleteSession`; that is inside better-auth. The Active Sessions list is not refreshed after a 2FA change ends the other sessions.

- **The required Playwright job now runs one test with real authentication (#843, P1.61)**: every spec ran with `BYPASS_AUTH`, so nothing required exercised the chain a real user depends on: better-auth signs a cookie, the backend verifies it, finds the session, and scopes data to that user. That is the class of failure behind #556.
  - **Config.** `frontend/playwright.real-auth.config.ts` starts its own backend (port 8001) and frontend (port 3001, `next dev`) with the bypass explicitly off, against their own database (`auto_author_e2e_real_auth`).
  - **Spec.** `frontend/src/e2e/real-auth/sign-up-to-protected-api.spec.ts` signs up through the page, is refused a session until the address is verified, opens the verification link the server emailed (read from the development server's output, since no provider is configured), then calls protected endpoints: the profile carries the id better-auth gave the user, a created book is owned by that id, the bare session token without its signature is refused, and a second user signed up the same way sees none of the first user's books. It also sends a weak password straight to the server and expects 400.
  - **CI.** The step runs in the required `E2E Tests (Playwright)` job after the bypassed suite. `scripts/test_real_auth_e2e_is_required.py` fails if the step is dropped or made conditional, if a bypass appears in its config, or if the spec stops being excluded from the bypassed run.
  - **Not covered.** One browser (Chromium), one flow. Password reset, 2FA and session revocation are not in it yet.

- **The backend verifies the session cookie's signature, and deployed environments accept only the `__Secure-` cookie (#795, P1.13)**: `get_session_token_from_cookies` took the part of the cookie before the dot and looked it up, ignoring the signature. The bare session token was therefore a working credential for seven days, and better-auth returns that token in the bodies of `/get-session` and `/list-sessions`, where any script on the page can read it.
  - **Signature.** The cookie is better-call's url-encoded `<token>.<signature>`, the signature being base64 HMAC-SHA256 of the token under `BETTER_AUTH_SECRET`. The backend now recomputes it and compares with `hmac.compare_digest`; a missing, forged or borrowed signature yields no token. A cookie that fails does not fall through to the other cookie name.
  - **Cookie name.** In a deployed environment (staging and production) only `__Secure-better-auth.session_token` is read. A browser will not let plain http or a script set a `__Secure-` cookie; the bare name can be planted by a sibling subdomain (cookie tossing, login CSRF). Development still accepts both.
  - **Tests.** Two cookies signed by the real library (better-call 1.4.0) with a fixed secret are checked in as fixtures, so the check is held to the library's algorithm and not to a copy of itself. `tests/session_cookies.py` signs cookies for the tests that drive the real session path; 25 of them had been sending a made-up signature.
  - **Deploy note.** The backend and the frontend must share one `BETTER_AUTH_SECRET`. On staging both read it from the same `.env`. If they ever differed, every request would be refused.
  - **Not covered.** better-auth still returns the session token in those response bodies; it is no longer enough on its own at the backend.

- **The password reset flow is hardened in four places (#794, P1.12)**:
  - **The password rule is enforced by the server.** Eight characters with an uppercase letter, a lowercase letter and a number was checked only in the browser, so a direct request could set `aaaaaaaa`. A better-auth `before` hook (`frontend/src/lib/auth-password-policy.ts`) now answers 400 to a new password that fails the rule at sign-up, reset and change. The rule itself moved to `frontend/src/lib/password-policy.ts`, shared by the hook and the page checklist. Sign-in is not checked: an existing account's password is not judged.
  - **Forgot-password says the same thing for every address.** The page no longer maps a server error to "No account found", never shows the server's own error text, and its success message reads "If an account exists for ..., we have sent it a password reset link."
  - **No address in the logs.** The reset hook logged the recipient on every send, and wrote the raw provider error on failure; it now logs neither. The backend's "created backend user record" line and a debug line in the session lookup dropped the address too.
  - **The name is escaped in the reset email.** The HTML part interpolated the user-chosen name as written, so markup in a name arrived from the genuine sender.
  - **Not covered.** An account that already has a weak password keeps it until it is next changed. The development console still prints whole emails, address included, when no provider is configured. Jest cannot load better-auth's server build, so the 400 itself is shown by the demo (#843).

- **A password reset or a two-factor change ends the account's other sessions (#793, P1.11)**: a session someone else already held survived the reset or the 2FA change that was meant to lock them out.
  - **Password reset.** `revokeSessionsOnPasswordReset` is on; better-auth's default is off. A reset deletes every session of the account.
  - **No session cookie cache.** With `session.cookieCache` on, `get-session` kept answering from the cookie for up to five minutes after the session row was gone, so a revoked session still looked signed in to the frontend while the backend answered 401. The cache is removed. Each session check is now one database lookup, which the backend already does on every request.
  - **Two-factor.** After 2FA is enabled or disabled from Settings, the page ends every other session (`revokeOtherSessions`). better-auth rotates only the session that made the change. If that call fails, the page says other devices are still signed in and where to end them.
  - **Not covered.** The two-factor revocation runs in the browser, after the change succeeds. A client that calls better-auth's endpoints directly and skips it leaves other sessions alive. Disabling 2FA still needs only the password. `get-session` answers `null` with 200 for a revoked session, not 401; the backend answers 401.

### 2026-10-11

- **The frontend reports whether sign-up email can be sent, and the staging probe checks it (#968, P1.10.1)**: since #792 nobody can finish signing up without the verification email, a missing provider is invisible to the user, and no health check knew about the frontend's email settings.
  - **Route.** `GET /api/health` on the frontend answers `{"status": "ok", "email": {"configured": true, "provider": "resend"}}`. It is public and unauthenticated, reads the environment on every request, and never returns the key or the from-address.
  - **One rule.** `emailProvider()` in `frontend/src/lib/email.ts` decides the answer: `resend` or `sendgrid` with an API key, `console` in development, otherwise none. A test holds it to what a send does, case by case, so the route cannot say "configured" for settings a send would reject.
  - **Probe.** `scripts/staging_health_probe.py` now fails unless that route says email is configured, so `staging-health.yml` raises its alert when a deploy or an edit to the box's `.env` loses the provider.
  - **Not covered.** "Configured" means a known provider and a key are present. It does not prove the key is valid or that mail is delivered.

- **A new account must verify its email address before it can sign in (#792, P1.10)**: anyone could register any address and was signed in at once, which allowed pre-account hijacking, throwaway accounts for AI quota, and receipts sent to addresses nobody had confirmed.
  - **better-auth.** `requireEmailVerification` is on. Sign-up creates the account and returns no session; sign-in for an unverified address answers 403 `EMAIL_NOT_VERIFIED` and mails a fresh link. The link is valid for one hour, verifies the address and signs the user in once; opening it again grants nothing.
  - **Email.** `frontend/src/lib/email.ts` has one delivery function behind both the reset and the verification email. The verification email carries no name, so nothing a user typed is put into its HTML. A failed send is not visible to the user: better-auth catches and logs it, and the page says "Check your email" either way. The trace is an `[email] Verification email NOT sent` line in the frontend's server log.
  - **Pages.** Sign-up shows "Check your email" with the address instead of opening the dashboard. Sign-in shows "Verify your email address to sign in. We have sent you a new link."
  - **Backend.** The first authenticated request creates the backend `users` record only when better-auth's user has `emailVerified: true`; otherwise 403. This is a second lock, for a session that exists despite the first.
  - **Existing accounts.** They never had a way to verify, so at startup the backend marks better-auth users that predate the requirement as verified (`grandfather_unverified_accounts`, idempotent). "Predate" means created before the backend first started with this change: that moment is written once to an `app_meta` document and reused, so a later restart never verifies an account created under the requirement. Without this every existing account, the staging E2E user included, would be locked out at its next sign-in. On a fresh database nothing predates the first start.
  - **Deploy note.** `EMAIL_SERVICE_PROVIDER`, `EMAIL_SERVICE_API_KEY` and `EMAIL_FROM_ADDRESS` are now required for sign-up to work at all, and a missing one is not visible from outside. Whether staging has them set was not checked from the box.
  - **Not covered.** There is no "resend" button: signing in is how a user gets another link. An expired or damaged link lands on the sign-in page with no message. A link opened from a sign-in resend lands on the home page, not the dashboard. Jest cannot load better-auth's server build, so the 403 is shown by the demo, not by an automated test (#843). Changing the email address of an existing account is not a feature, so it is not covered.

- **An account can download its own data (#790, P1.8)**: `/privacy` section 9 lists "receive a copy of your data in a portable format" as a right, and nothing produced that copy.
  - **Endpoint.** `GET /users/me/export` returns one JSON attachment: an allowlist of profile fields from the users doc, every book the account owns with its chapter content, and every question, answer and rating that carries the account's id. It takes no id, so there is nothing to tamper with; each query filters on the session's own `auth_id`. Limited to 10 per hour, sent with `Cache-Control: no-store`, every date marked as UTC. A book's `collaborators` list is left out, because it would name other accounts.
  - **Profile page.** A "Download my data" link next to Delete Account points at the endpoint, and the browser saves the attachment.
  - **Copy.** `/privacy` section 9 says the download exists, names what is in it and what is not, and that the rest is available on request.
  - **Not covered.** The audit log, chapter access log and usage counters are not in the file, nor are uploaded image files (their URLs are), nor anything better-auth or Stripe holds. The response is built in memory, not streamed. A failed download shows the API's JSON error page, because the control is a plain link. The export is not written to the audit log. Counsel has not seen the new wording (#791).

- **A deactivated account keeps its Terms acceptance record for the 18 months (#960, P1.7.1)**: #789 recorded which Terms version an account accepted and when, but the fields were not on the #954 retained list, so deleting the account erased them. A user who then disputed a charge saying they never agreed could not be answered. The owner decided on 2026-10-11 to keep them.
  - **What changed.** `terms_version` and `terms_accepted_at` joined `RETAINED_FIELDS` in `backend/app/db/user.py`. `/privacy` section 8 names them in its list of what is kept, and `docs/api-profile-endpoints.md` lists them.
  - **Nulls are left out.** The backend writes `None` for an account created before #789. The deactivation pipeline now drops a null field as well as a missing one, so such an account's retained record carries no empty acceptance entry.
  - **Not covered.** An account deactivated between #789 and this change has already lost the record. The Terms did not change, so `TERMS_VERSION` is still `2026-10-10`. Counsel has not seen the new wording (#791).

- **Sign-up records the Terms version accepted and when, and shows the notice above the button (#789, P1.7)**: the agreement line sat below the submit button, and nothing recorded that a user had agreed or to which text.
  - **Where it is written.** better-auth stamps `termsVersion` and `termsAcceptedAt` on the user row it creates, in the request that creates the account (`frontend/src/lib/auth-terms.ts`, wired in `auth.ts` as two `additionalFields` and a `user.create.before` hook). The backend copies them onto its own `users` doc as `terms_version` and `terms_accepted_at` when it creates that doc, the same way it already copies email and name.
  - **The browser cannot choose them.** Both fields are `input: false`: better-auth answers 400 "termsVersion is not allowed to be set" to a sign-up body that carries one, and the hook overwrites whatever reaches it.
  - **One version constant.** `TERMS_VERSION` in `frontend/src/lib/constants/legal.ts` is the ISO date the Terms last changed. `/terms` builds its "Last updated" line from it, so the recorded version and the displayed date cannot drift. Changing the Terms copy means changing that constant.
  - **Notice.** The "By creating an account, you agree to our Terms of Service and Privacy Policy" line now renders above the Sign Up button. The wording is unchanged.
  - **Copy.** `/privacy` section 2 lists the acceptance record under account information; its date is now October 11, 2026. The Terms did not change, so `TERMS_VERSION` is still `2026-10-10`.
  - **Not covered.** Accounts created before this change have no value in either place (`None` on a `users` doc created after it), and nothing is backfilled: there is no record that they accepted. Nothing asks an existing user to accept again when `TERMS_VERSION` changes. The two fields are not in the record a deactivated account keeps (#954), so they are erased with the rest. Jest cannot load better-auth's server build, so the unit test checks what `auth.ts` hands the library; the real sign-up is in the demo.

### 2026-10-10

- **Deleting an account deactivates it and keeps the account and billing record for 18 months (#954, P1.6.1)**: deletion erased everything at once, so a complaint or chargeback arriving afterwards had nothing to be answered with. The owner decided on 2026-10-10 what is kept, for how long, and that support can erase earlier.
  - **What `DELETE /users/me` does now.** It still cancels the subscription, bulk-deletes every book with its content, deletes the avatar and removes the better-auth identity. The users record is replaced by `auth_id`, `deleted_at`, `purge_at` (548 days out) and a `retained` sub-document: email, first, last and display name, `created_at`, plan, `stripe_customer_id` and `cancelled_subscription`. It is an allowlist written by one `$replaceWith`, so a field added to users later is erased by default.
  - **Why `retained` is nested.** A top-level `email` sits on the unique index. When the same address signs up again, auto-create collides and the #765 resolver (`release_email`) unsets the email on the old record, which would silently strip the thing the record is kept for. Nested, the record neither blocks the new account nor is attached to it.
  - **`deleted_at` is still the one closed-account marker.** The session resolver, `update_user`, the Stripe webhook and `create_book` already reject a record that has it, so none of them changed. `purge_at` is what tells a deactivated record from a #784 tombstone.
  - **The plan the user was paying for.** `cancel_subscription_for_deletion` resets plan and subscription before the record is written (#764), so it now stamps `cancelled_subscription` (`id`, `plan`, `status`) first. Without it the retained record of a paying user would read "free".
  - **Purge.** `purge_expired_users` is one pipeline `update_many` that turns records past `purge_at` into the tombstone. The lifespan runs it at startup and then once a day, so it does not wait for a deploy. Not a TTL index: that deletes the document, and the tombstone is what stops a surviving session from re-creating the account.
  - **Early erasure.** An admin `DELETE /users/{auth_id}` on a deactivated record erases it and writes a `user_erase` audit row; on an active account it deactivates. That route also now records the admin as the actor instead of the target.
  - **Late Stripe events.** An event that resolves to a deactivated or erased record is acknowledged with `{"status": "account_closed"}` and writes nothing. It used to answer `no_matching_user` and log "matches no user", which was untrue once the record could be found. No replay marker is stored, as before.
  - **Copy.** The profile dialog, `/privacy` sections 3, 8 and 9 and `/terms` section 7 say the same thing. Section 8 lists the retained fields one by one and section 3 names complaints and payment disputes as a legitimate interest. `/terms` section 7 also says deleting cancels a paid subscription immediately, with no paid access for the rest of the period, which section 6 alone did not make clear. The confirm button reads "Deactivate account and delete books".
  - **Checked.** A fresh agent compared every sentence of the new copy with the code: none false; the retained list gained the account identifier and deletion date, and the API doc gained the 502 and a corrected admin-route section. Demo: `docs/demos/2026-10-10-issue-954-account-deactivation.md`. `codex review --base main` found nothing; opencode/GLM stalled with no output.
  - **Not covered.** Reactivation (books are gone at deactivation). Audit rows still expire after 90 days, so the 18-month record carries no activity history. The Stripe Customer is untouched at every stage (#953). Accounts deleted before this change are already tombstones and stay that way. Counsel has not confirmed the 18 months or the wording (#791).

- **Privacy Policy names its subprocessors and covers lawful basis, transfers, training, cookies, children and rights (#788, P1.6)**: the policy named no recipient while the app sends data to OpenAI, Stripe, MongoDB Atlas, Sentry, an email provider and a file store, and it was silent on six topics a policy has to cover.
  - **Subprocessor table.** `frontend/src/lib/constants/subprocessors.ts` is the list; section 5 of `/privacy` renders one row per entry (provider, purpose, data it receives). Both email providers (Resend, SendGrid) and both file stores (S3, Cloudinary) are listed because which one runs is a deploy-time setting. The browser's speech service has a row with no credential: voice input sends audio from the browser straight to its vendor.
  - **Tripwire.** `subprocessors.test.ts` sweeps every setting in `backend/app/core/config.py`, every `os.getenv`/`os.environ` read under `backend/app`, and every `process.env` read in shipped frontend source. Any name ending in `_KEY`, `_KEY_ID`, `_SECRET`, `_DSN`, `_URI`, `_URL`, `_TOKEN` or `_PASSWORD` must be claimed by an entry's `envKeys` or by the first-party list (our signing secret, our own origins, and the dev seed script's password). `AI_BASE_URL` is claimed by the OpenAI row and `DATABASE_URL` by the MongoDB row. A claimed key that no longer exists also fails. It cannot see an integration that takes no credential, or a provider swapped by changing a value: `AI_BASE_URL` carries a comment saying so in `config.py` and `backend/.env.example`, because section 4 describes OpenAI's API terms and is wrong for any other provider.
  - **New sections.** Lawful bases (in section 3), training use (section 4), international transfers (6), cookies and local storage (7), children (10). Section 9 lists the rights one by one, including restriction, objection and complaint. Retention is now section 8 and says that deleting an account cancels the subscription while past payment records stay with the payment processor (the #784 finding).
  - **Decisions taken without the owner, for counsel (#791) to confirm.** The children threshold is 16; the Terms have no age clause. Transfers rely on the Standard Contractual Clauses in the providers' data processing terms, which assumes those terms are accepted. "Not used to train its models by default" describes the AI provider's API terms and assumes the account has not opted in to data sharing.
  - **Not covered.** The hosting provider is not named, because there is no production host yet (#782). The Sentry row says error reports can contain fragments of content, which is true until #808 scrubs them. The policy still lists a portable copy of your data as a right while no self-serve export exists (#790). Section 7 says signing out does not clear the browser's backup copies, which is the behaviour #855 tracks.

- **Audit log rows expire after 90 days, carry no titles, and reads are not logged (#787, P1.5)**: `audit_logs` kept every row forever, with the IP address and user-agent of the request and, on book create and delete, the book's title. It had no index, and it gained a row on every book view and every `/users/me` fetch.
  - **Retention is per row.** `create_audit_log` stamps `expires_at` (timestamp + 90 days) and a TTL index with `expireAfterSeconds=0` reaps the row. 90 days matches `chapter_access_logs`.
  - **Renewal-consent rows never expire.** `billing.renewal_consent` (#770) is the evidence that a subscriber agreed to the renewal terms, and it has to outlive the subscription. Those rows are written without `expires_at`, which the TTL monitor skips. A TTL on `timestamp` could not do this: a partial index cannot express "action is not X". `RETAINED_ACTIONS` in `app/db/audit_log.py` is the list, and a test fails if the billing constant is renamed away from it.
  - **Existing rows are backfilled at startup.** `ensure_audit_log_indexes` runs from the lifespan, creates the TTL index and an `(actor_id, timestamp)` index, then stamps every row that has no `expires_at` and is not a consent row. A row with a missing or non-date `timestamp` counts from the time of the backfill. It runs on every boot and matches nothing after the first. Like the other startup indexes it logs a failure and lets the app start.
  - **No titles.** Book titles are gone from the `book_create` and `book_delete` rows (DAO and endpoint), and the chapter title from `generate_chapter_draft`. Rows written before this still hold them until they expire.
  - **Decision: reads are not audited.** The `book_view` and `profile_view` rows are removed, not sampled. Nothing reads `audit_logs`, a read changes no state, and `chapter_access_logs` already records reading activity with its own 90-day TTL. A sample of views would be evidence of nothing in particular, so the choice was all or none.
  - **Privacy Policy.** Section 5 states the 90-day retention, that deleting an account does not remove audit rows sooner, and the consent-record exception.
  - **Also removed.** The top-level `ip_address` field, which was always `null`; the address lives in `details.ip_address`.
  - **Not covered.** Consent rows keep their IP address and user-agent with no end date, including after the account is deleted. Upload file names are still recorded on `avatar_upload` and `cover_image_upload` rows, for 90 days. A failed TTL build is only a log line; `/health` does not report it.
  - **Unrelated fix in the same PR.** `test_migration_account_issuer.py` read better-auth's source for `createLocalAccountIssuer`, which 1.7.7 (#919) removed along with `account.issuer`. The test failed on any checkout whose `frontend/node_modules` matched the lockfile, which blocked every backend commit there; CI's backend job installs no `node_modules`, so it skipped. It now skips when the installed library has no account issuer and still asserts the `local:` namespace on a version that has one.
  - **Verified.** Real MongoDB throughout: both indexes after the real lifespan, the stamp and the consent exemption, the backfill over five row shapes, and a client pointed at a closed port for the failure path. An API test with the real `audit_request` creates, views and deletes a book and string-scans every audit row for the title. A source scan covers the call sites that need an AI call to reach.

- **Account deletion deletes in bulk, one command per collection (#786, P1.4)**: `delete_all_user_books` called `delete_book` once per book, and each call cost ten round trips: an unprojected read of the whole book, a replica-set probe, five deletes, a question read, a user update and an audit insert. A 2,649-book account sent 26,492 commands: 17 seconds against a local mongod, and past the proxy timeout on staging according to the launch review.
  - **One cascade, two callers.** `_delete_book_internal(book_id)` became `_delete_books_internal(book_ids)` and filters with `$in`. `delete_book` passes one id; the account cascade passes them all. The same 2,649-book account now sends 11 commands, and the count does not change with the number of books.
  - **No transaction on the account cascade.** The per-book path wrapped each book in one on a replica set. A single transaction over a whole account would outlive Mongo's 60 second transaction limit on exactly the accounts this is for. Children still go first and books last, so a failure leaves every book in place, the endpoint answers 500, the account stays active and the delete can be retried. A single-book delete is unchanged: transactional on a replica set.
  - **One audit row per account, not one per book.** The cascade writes a single `delete_books` row with the counts. The 2,649 `book_delete` rows it replaces each kept a deleted account's book title (#787).
  - **Question ids go in chunks of 100,000.** Responses and ratings are keyed by question id alone, and a single `$in` filter is capped at 16MB. Past 100,000 questions the cascade sends one more pair of deletes per chunk.
  - **The single-book delete reads three fields.** Its ownership read pulled every chapter's HTML to delete the book.
  - **Not covered.** Cover files are still one storage call per book that has a cover. No book can have one yet (#941).
  - **Verified.** `test_cascade_delete_atomic.py` attaches a pymongo command listener and asserts the exact command set for 3 and for 250 books. The file also passes against a single-node replica set, which is what runs the transactional single-book path.

- **Image URLs are server-written, and storage deletes are scoped to the owner (#797, P1.3.1)**: `cover_image_url` and `avatar_url` were client-writable, and the stored value is what the upload and delete paths hand to storage as the file to remove. A user could point their book at another tenant's object and have it deleted by uploading a cover, deleting the book or deleting the account.
  - **The fields are no longer request input.** `BookCreate` and `BookUpdate` ignore `cover_image_url`; the shipped frontend sent it on every metadata save, so a 422 would have broken saves for cached bundles. `UserUpdate` is already a forbid-list (#244), so `avatar_url` there is a 422. `BookResponse` still returns the cover URL.
  - **Every delete names its owner.** `delete_cover_image(book_id, ...)` and `delete_profile_picture(user_id, ...)` pass the provider a required prefix, `cover_images/{book_id}/` or `profile_pictures/{user_id}/`. S3 and Cloudinary parse the URL with `urlparse`, require their exact host and refuse a key outside the prefix. Local deletes require the name the service wrote for that owner, `<owner>_<uuid hex>[_thumb].<ext>`. All four call sites pass the id: both upload routes, `delete_book` and `delete_user`.
  - **Cover upload persists first.** The route deleted the old cover before storing the new URL and ignored a failed `update_book`. It now mirrors the avatar route: store the URLs, then delete the old files, and remove the new files if the write fails or matches no book.
  - **Cover upload is rate limited** at 5 per 60 seconds.
  - **A side effect that was a bug.** The metadata form sent `cover_image_url: ''` on every save, and the update routes wrote any non-null value, so saving a book's title cleared the URL of an uploaded cover. Ignoring the field ends that.
  - **Frontend.** The two "Cover Image URL" inputs are removed. A typed URL never rendered: no remote image host is configured, and staging's image optimizer answers 400 for one.
  - **Existing data.** A stored URL that is not the owner's own object is now left alone on replace and delete. That includes any external URL typed into the old field.

- **Deleting a book or an account deletes its uploaded files (#785, P1.3)**: cover and thumbnail files were only ever removed when a new cover replaced them, and the avatar never, so every deleted book and account left its images in storage while the Privacy Policy calls deletion permanent.
  - **Two call sites, not five.** `delete_book` deletes the cover and thumbnail, which covers `DELETE /books/{id}` and the account cascade (`delete_all_user_books` calls it per book). `delete_user` deletes the avatar, which covers `DELETE /users/me` and the admin `DELETE /users/{auth_id}`. It now uses `find_one_and_replace` (and `find_one_and_delete` on the hard path) because #784's tombstone erases `avatar_url`: the returned pre-image is the last place the URL exists.
  - **Database first, file second.** A file cannot roll back, so it goes only after the delete has committed. A failed cascade or a non-owner request leaves the files alone.
  - **Best-effort, and never silent.** A storage failure is logged and the delete still succeeds. `delete_cover_image` now handles each file on its own, so a failed cover delete no longer skips the thumbnail. S3 and Cloudinary answer `False` for a URL they don't recognise (a local-mode URL left from before cloud storage was configured, say); both helpers now log that as a warning with the URL instead of discarding it. A file left behind this way is unreferenced, and nothing sweeps it.
  - **Not covered.** The stored URLs are still client-writable (#797), so these two paths delete whatever URL the record holds, exactly as the replace-on-upload paths already do.
  - **Verified.** `test_deletion_removes_uploads.py` uses real files and real MongoDB, storage pinned to local mode: the three routes (the book one after a real upload), both DAOs, a failed cascade, a non-owner, and a storage root that cannot be created. The cloud branch runs against an in-test stand-in for the provider: both files deleted, a raising cover delete, and a refused URL.

### 2026-10-09

- **Account deletion erases the user record to a tombstone (#784, P1.2)**: the Privacy Policy calls deletion permanent, but the soft-deleted `users` doc kept names, bio, avatar URL, preferences and Stripe ids (#763 had already removed the better-auth `user`/`account`/`session`/`twoFactor` docs and the email). `delete_user` now `replace_one`s the doc with `{auth_id, deleted_at}`. Replacing instead of unsetting means a field added later is erased without anyone remembering to list it.
  - **The tombstone stays empty.** `update_user` no longer matches a doc with `deleted_at`, so an admin `PUT /users/{id}` gets 404 and nothing can `$set` data back. The Stripe webhook treats a tombstone as `no_matching_user`: with the customer id erased, a late event reaches the account through the `metadata.auth_id` fallback and used to be free to write `plan`, customer and subscription ids back. `create_book`'s `book_ids` push carries the same filter, for a request that authenticated just before the delete and lands after it.
  - **Still locked out.** The session resolver rejects `deleted_at` as it does `is_active: False`, which covers a better-auth user row that outlives the tombstone (identity erasure failing after the replace).
  - **Not covered.** Uploaded avatar and cover *files* (#785), `audit_logs` retention (#787), and docs soft-deleted before this change (`is_active: false`, staging test accounts only — production has never run).
  - **Verified.** `test_deletion_erases_the_account_to_a_tombstone` signs in through a real better-auth session, fills the profile, deletes, then string-scans every document in every collection for the email, both names and the Stripe customer id, with a vacuity check that the scan finds them beforehand.

- **Checkout collects tax details, with Stripe Tax behind a flag (#783, P1.1)**: every Checkout Session now requires a billing address and offers tax-ID collection, with `customer_update={"address": "auto", "name": "auto"}`. Stripe refuses both on an existing Customer otherwise, and this checkout always passes one. `automatic_tax` follows the new `STRIPE_AUTOMATIC_TAX` setting, off by default, because Stripe rejects every checkout with automatic tax on an account where Stripe Tax isn't activated. The operator enables it after registering in the Dashboard. The renewal disclosure now says "plus any applicable tax"; its version is bumped to 2026-10-09, so the #770 hash check re-prompts consent. Terms section 6 gained a tax clause and a new last-updated date. Tests: `test_billing_checkout_tax.py` and the Terms page test.

- **AI provider and models are configuration (#917, P0.35)**: the backend can call any OpenAI-compatible endpoint, and each class of flow gets its own model. With nothing set, requests are byte-for-byte what they were before.
  - **Settings.** `AI_BASE_URL` (empty means api.openai.com), `AI_API_KEY`, `AI_MODEL_DEFAULT` (`gpt-4`: analysis, questions, enhance, transform, transcription cleanup), `AI_MODEL_LONG_OUTPUT` (`gpt-4o`: drafts and TOC), plus `AI_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS_DEFAULT`/`_LONG` (4000/8000). The caps clamp each class's `max_tokens` in `_make_openai_request`, so a model with a smaller output limit is never sent a value it rejects. They replace the `DRAFT_GENERATION_MODEL`/`DRAFT_MAX_COMPLETION_TOKENS` constants. They reach the container through the backend's `env_file:`.
  - **Key handling.** `AI_API_KEY` takes precedence over `OPENAI_API_KEY`. When `AI_BASE_URL` is set it is the only key used, so the OpenAI key is never sent to a third-party endpoint, and `/health` reports `AI_API_KEY` if it is missing. A missing key becomes a placeholder rather than `""`, which the SDK rejects at import and the backend would not boot. The SDK's `OPENAI_BASE_URL` env fallback is pinned out (codex review), so it can't carry the key elsewhere either. `AI_API_KEY` joins the frontend backend-secret leak guards (`scripts/test_frontend_env_allowlist.py` and the deploy's container `env` check).
  - **Billing errors.** HTTP 402, which Ollama cloud and OpenRouter send when credit runs out, maps to `AI_PROVIDER_QUOTA_EXHAUSTED` like OpenAI's 429 `insufficient_quota` (#775).
  - **Parsers on real open-model output.** Fixtures in `backend/tests/fixtures/ai_provider_outputs/nemotron-3-nano-30b/` were captured from NVIDIA's Nemotron 3 Nano through the app's own prompts. They exposed two bugs that would also hit gpt-4 output formatted the same way. A `SUGGESTIONS:` block written as a list on the following lines was dropped. Markdown-bold numbered questions (`1. **Why...?**`) turned into five junk questions, one of them `**?`. Both parsers now strip list markers (`LIST_MARKER`) and emphasis, and `1.5x` is no longer read as item 1.
  - **Truncation everywhere.** Summary analysis, clarifying questions and chapter questions now refuse a `finish_reason: length` completion (`_untruncated_text`), as drafts and TOC already did (#774). The live demo caught it: Nemotron spent all 1000 analysis tokens on hidden reasoning in 4 of 4 runs, and the empty answer was parsed into a confident "not ready, 0.5" verdict.
  - **Verified.** `test_ai_provider_config.py` pins the default request params. It also runs a real local HTTP stub to check the configured models, key and path, the per-class caps, 402 handling and `finish_reason: length` truncation. Mutation-checked. Provider differences (reasoning models spending `max_tokens`, error shapes) are in `docs/STAGING-DEPLOYMENT.md` under "AI provider settings".

### 2026-10-04

- **Staging E2E runs after each staging deploy instead of every 6 hours (#916, P0.27.1)**: the schedule re-tested a build last deployed 2026-08-28 and spent about 10 live OpenAI calls per run (up to 3x on a red run through CI retries), so a scheduled run could only fail on something outside the code, such as #775's empty quota.
  - **Triggers.** `e2e-staging-tests.yml` drops `schedule:` for `workflow_run` on "Deploy Staging (Containers)". It runs only when that deploy concluded `success` and came from this repository, and it checks out `workflow_run.head_sha`, the commit the deploy ran from. Dispatch and the `e2e-staging` PR label work as before. Known limit: a rollback deploy of an older `image_tag` still checks out the dispatch commit, so its specs can be newer than the build.
  - **AI spend.** The live-AI journey test and the #54 test run with `retries: 0` via `test.describe.configure`; every other test keeps CI's 2. The #54 test now seeds its book, summary and TOC through the API (`seedBookWithToc` in `journey.helpers.ts`, built on the TOC helper moved out of `edge-cases.spec.ts`). Its one remaining AI call is the chapter question generation, since an answer needs a real question id. That call is now logged like the wizard's (#775).
  - **Alerting.** The #776 `alert` job fires on post-deploy runs and on dispatches from the default branch (so a green rerun closes the issue), never on labelled-PR runs, and the title says "Post-deploy". The label stays `ci-alert:staging-e2e`.
  - **Health probe.** New `staging-health.yml` runs every 6 hours: `scripts/staging_health_probe.py` (stdlib, 3 attempts) needs `/api/v1/health` to answer 200 `{"status": "healthy"}` and the frontend root to answer 200. A failure opens one `ci-alert:staging-health` issue (`[P0.27.1] Staging health probe is failing`) via `scripts/ci-alert-issue.sh`, and the next healthy run closes it. No AI, no auth, no secrets.
  - **Verified.** `scripts/test_staging_triggers.py` pins the trigger, the deploy workflow's name, the green-deploy and checkout conditions and the probe workflow's wiring. It also runs the probe against a local HTTP server: healthy, 503 unhealthy, 200 without a verdict, frontend 502, one transient failure, unreachable. Locally, the #54 chain passed against a real backend + frontend with an OpenAI wire stub, which recorded exactly one AI call. The probe passed against live staging.

### 2026-10-02

- **One frontend image per environment, with no staging fallbacks (#779, P0.31)**:
  - **The bug.** Next inlines `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` into the client bundle at build time. `build-images.yml` baked repo-level vars with staging hostnames as the `||` fallback and tagged the one result `sha-*` and `staging`. A production frontend could not be built without repointing staging, and an unset var shipped staging hosts silently. No repo or environment vars existed, so every image so far came from those fallbacks.
  - **The fix.**
    - The matrix builds one frontend per environment. Staging is the only one today; production is one more matrix entry, added with its deploy (#782). Each job runs in its GitHub environment with `deployment: false`, so `vars.*` are that environment's values and no fake deployment is recorded.
    - Build args have no fallbacks, and a publishing run fails closed if `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL` or `NEXT_PUBLIC_BETTER_AUTH_URL` is unset. `NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_DSN` (optional) and `NEXT_PUBLIC_ENVIRONMENT` (the environment's name) are now build args too.
    - Frontend tags are `sha-<short>-staging` (also floated as `staging`). The backend keeps `sha-<short>`, which stays the deploy's `image_tag`, and the staging overlay pulls `<tag>-staging`.
    - The smoke test greps the image's `.next/static` for its own API host. `docker-compose.build.yml` requires the values instead of defaulting to staging.
    - PR builds name no environment. They prove the Dockerfile builds and publish nothing.
  - **Verified.** `scripts/test_frontend_image_per_environment.py` has 9 tests. Restoring a fallback, re-enabling deployments, dropping a variant's environment, defaulting the compose arg, un-suffixing the overlay or removing a Dockerfile ARG each fails a test. Two local `docker build`s were checked: each `.next/static` holds its own API host in 7 files and the other environment's in 0. The workflow's own fail-closed script exits 1 per missing var. `actionlint` is clean on both touched workflows.
  - **Setup.** `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL` and `NEXT_PUBLIC_BETTER_AUTH_URL` are set on the `staging` environment. Nothing here needs a `production` environment.

- **PDF export no longer parses author text as ReportLab markup (#752, P0.4)**:
  - **The bug.** Title, subtitle, author name, description, genre, audience, chapter title and description, and every body paragraph went to `Paragraph()` unescaped (only `author_bio` was escaped). Text like `A </i> B` crashed the export, and `<img src="/server/path">` embedded a server-local file, with an `UnidentifiedImageError` that doubled as a file-existence oracle.
  - **The fix.** One helper, `_pdf_text`, escapes every user-derived string before `Paragraph()`. At import, `rl_config.trustedSchemes = ['data']` and `trustedHosts = []`, a pin, not a fix: reportlab 5.0.1 already refuses URL image sources (`http`, `file://`, `data:`) with its defaults, measured both ways, and the lock keeps an upgrade that loosens them from turning `<img>` into SSRF. It does not cover a plain filesystem path, which reportlab opens before any scheme check, so the escape is the sole guard against embedding a server-local file. Any new `Paragraph()` call must go through `_pdf_text`. It is PDF-only: python-docx escapes for itself and Markdown has nothing to escape, so the DOCX and Markdown builders pass raw values (escaping there exported `Sci-Fi & Fantasy` as `Sci-Fi &amp; Fantasy`).
  - **Verified.** A new test exports hostile text through `export_book`, extracts the text from the uncompressed content streams, and asserts it appears literally with no `/Subtype /Image`. Mutation checks: unescaped body paragraph fails the export test; dropping the scheme lock fails the config test.

- **Scheduled staging E2E failures now open a GitHub issue (#776, P0.28)**: the old notify step only fired when a Slack or Discord secret existed, and neither does, so the suite sat red from 2026-09-20 with nobody told. That step is gone. A new `alert` job in `e2e-staging-tests.yml` (scheduled runs only, `issues: write` on that job alone, no new secret) calls `scripts/ci-alert-issue.sh`: a failure opens one `ci-alert:staging-e2e` issue or comments on the one already open, and the next green run comments and closes it. The helper takes `ALERT_LABEL`/`ALERT_TITLE`/`RUN_URL`, so P1.29 can reuse it unchanged. `scripts/test_ci_alert_issue.py` covers create, dedupe, close, no-op recovery and the job's permissions. Caveat: a just-created issue takes a second or two to appear in the label lookup, irrelevant for 6-hourly runs.
- **The Stripe webhook records an event as processed only after applying it (#769, P0.21)**:
  - **The bug.** The handler inserted the event's idempotency marker first and deleted it again only on `Exception`. A crash, a redeploy or a `CancelledError` between the claim and the user write left the marker behind, so Stripe's retry got `replay` and the plan change was lost until the marker's 30-day TTL expired.
  - **The fix.** `stripe_events.is_event_processed` checks for a replay, the event is applied, and only then does `mark_event_processed` record it, for `processed` results only. A worker that dies anywhere before that line leaves no marker, so the retry re-applies. Re-applying is safe: the write is a plain `$set` guarded by the `stripe_event_created` filter, so a retry that arrives after a newer event is rejected as stale. `no_matching_user` and `not_current_subscription` record nothing, as before, so a dashboard Resend still reprocesses them. `stale_event` records nothing either, so a redelivery re-checks the watermark instead of trusting it never moved back (raised by the opencode/GLM review). `unmark_event` is gone because nothing releases a marker any more.
  - **Tests on real Mongo.** The fixture `subscription_event` now always stamps `created`, as Stripe does, from a counter so each event built is strictly newer and no test flips on a second boundary. Every signed-webhook test now runs the ordering guard; before, none did. That exposed #768's resend test, which passed only because its events had no timestamp: with real ones, B's event must postdate A's deletion to apply (an older one is `stale_event`, #768's per-user watermark limit), so it now says so. The new `test_stripe_webhook_delivery.py` covers these cases:
    - A `BaseException` raised by the user write. This is the redeploy shape that `except Exception` never caught. Stripe's retry applies pro.
    - A crash after the write but before the marker re-applies, and the next duplicate is a `replay`.
    - `evt_old` after `evt_new` returns `stale_event` and leaves the plan and watermark alone.
    - A same-second event still applies.
    - A newer event landing between the lookup and the write still wins.
    The two fake-DAO ordering tests in `test_hardening_352.py` were replaced by these.
  - **Known limit.** Two concurrent deliveries of the same event id can both pass the check and both apply. The write is identical, so the only trace is a second audit-log row.
- **The billing portal is pinned to a cancel-at-period-end Configuration made in code (#771, P0.23)**:
  - **The bug.** `POST /billing/portal` passed no `configuration`, so whether a subscriber could cancel at all depended on unversioned Stripe dashboard settings, and the #770 disclosure ("cancellation takes effect at the end of the current period") had nothing enforcing it.
  - **The fix.** `_get_or_create_portal_config` finds our active portal Configuration by metadata (`app` + `portal_config_version`) or creates it with `features.subscription_cancel` enabled in `at_period_end` mode, plus payment-method update and invoice history. Creation carries a fixed idempotency key, and the id is cached per process. Every portal session now passes `configuration=`. Bump `portal_config_version` to roll out a changed Configuration.
  - **Verified.** `test_billing_portal.py` now runs the real Stripe SDK against a local HTTP wire stub (`stripe.api_base`), replacing the SDK monkeypatch. It asserts the create call's form fields, the `configuration` on the session call, and reuse without re-creating. The stub caught an SDK trap (`StripeObject.metadata` has no `.get`) that a monkeypatch would have hidden.
  - **Owner homework.** Confirm in the Stripe dashboard (test, then live) that the portal shows the Configuration and cancelling in it leaves the subscription active until period end.
- **Staging real-auth E2E red since 2026-09-20: cause found, and an empty OpenAI account is no longer reported as a rate limit (#775, P0.27)**:
  - **The cause is not code.** The last green run (2026-09-20T18:11Z) and the first red run (2026-09-21T00:29Z) used the same commit against the same staging deploy (last deploy 2026-08-28). A branch-dispatched run with response logging captured `toc-readiness` 200 `meets_minimum_requirements: true` and `generate-questions` **429 `AI_RATE_LIMIT`** on every attempt. `analyze-summary` returned 503 `AI_ANALYSIS_FAILED`, and the wizard ignores that one. The assertion's "summary may have been judged NOT_READY" guess was wrong for 12 days. The 429 comes from OpenAI itself after 3 tries, at roughly 8 calls every 6 hours, so it is almost certainly OpenAI's `insufficient_quota` (no credit). The fix is OWNER-HOMEWORK: add credit to the OpenAI account behind staging's key.
