The migration is the codemod plus hand corrections, each found by measurement rather than review. **The rendered result was the gate**: a Chromium computed-style diff of all **975** class strings in shipped source, built with v3 from `main` and v4 from the branch, in both themes, across 64 properties. That ended with **zero unexplained differences**. What remains is spelling (`rounded-full` as `calc(infinity*1px)`, transforms as the separate `translate`/`rotate`/`scale` properties, `outline-hidden` as `outline-style:none`), the typography plugin's oklch grays (about one channel unit), and two shadcn classes that didn't exist in v3 now rendering as designed (`shadow-2xs` on inputs, `rounded-xs` on dialog close buttons). **Theme:** v3's palette is pinned as hex in `@theme` (v4's oklch palette is different colours, and every published ratio was measured on the hex), and the brand stays `rgb(79 70 229)`. **What the codemod got wrong:** it repainted the brand near-black (a later `@theme inline` won); froze `destructive` light in dark; moved `:root`/`.dark` into `@layer utilities` and left #610's override unlayered (in v4 an unlayered rule beats every utility); rewrote guard-test fixtures and the English word "ring"; changed two `variant="outline"` props to `"outline-solid"`; left v3's removed `bg-opacity-50` (a solid black mobile overlay) and `ring-opacity-50`; and never opened `editor.css`, which failed the production build until it `@reference`s the theme. The mechanical `/50` conversion of `ring-opacity-50` would also have halved a dark ring v3 painted opaque, because `dark:ring-blue-700` reset the opacity in v3; the diff caught that. **tailwind-merge 3** (3.6.0; 3.7.0 was a day old): diffing v2 against v3 over 3945 static class strings found only the opacity sites. **Guards:** the theme suite read `tailwind.config.js` and `tailwindcss/colors`; both are gone or wrong under v4. They now read `@theme` through `helpers/palette.ts`, the emission guard builds the real `globals.css` with the v4 CLI, and a tripwire rejects v3 `-opacity-N` utilities. Mutation-checked: dropping `--color-muted`, repainting the brand, reverting the destructive fill, lightening a pinned green, and reformatting the palette pin are each caught. **Also:** `autoprefixer` removed (v4 prefixes itself); Dependabot's build group names `@tailwindcss/*`, and its config test now matches globs with `fnmatch`, where `==` had rejected the correct glob exclusion outright.

### 2026-09-13

- **Opacity variants of every `var()` theme colour emitted no CSS, and fixing that exposed a failing focus ring (#697, P2.28)**: found while measuring the input-border consequence of #695. `tailwind.config.js` configured ten colours as a bare `var(--x)`, and Tailwind v3 cannot put an opacity modifier on a colour it can't parse. So `<token>/<n>` produced **no rule**, the same mechanism #682 found for `destructive`. A sweep of shipped source built with the real config found **14 classes, all 14 missing**, while controls in the same build emitted (`bg-destructive/10`, `bg-primary/90`). Visible effects: secondary `Button`/`Badge` had **no hover state** (`hover:bg-secondary/80`, 8 files), muted tints were transparent, the dark `Input` fill was missing, and `ring-ring/50` fell back to Tailwind's default blue. #682's channel form doesn't fit oklch tokens, so they are now `color-mix(in oklch, var(--x) calc(<alpha-value> * 100%), transparent)`. In Chromium, opaque classes compute identically in both themes. `destructive-utilities-emitted.test.ts` became `opacity-utilities-emitted.test.ts` and builds **every** opacity-modified colour class, not just destructive; its old comment called that widening "a bigger job than the bug warrants", and this was the evidence it wasn't. **The fix alone would have weakened focus.** `ring-ring/50` over `outline-none` is the entire focus indicator on buttons, inputs, selects, textareas and badges. shadcn's `--ring` at half strength measured **1.54:1** light and **1.88:1** dark, weaker than the accidental blue (1.83 / 2.13), and all four are under 1.4.11's 3:1. `--ring` now uses the `--primary` lightness (0.205 light, 0.922 dark): **3.34:1** and **4.16:1** at worst, on `--muted`. Every other `--ring` consumer is a focus state, so a darker ring only strengthens them. `focus-ring-contrast.test.ts` prices every alpha the call sites use on every surface a control sits on. Six mutations are caught, including sweeps blinded to `/alpha` and narrowed back to destructive.

- **Every bordered surface drew a hard black (light) or white (dark) edge instead of the hairline token (#695, P2.27)**: found while diffing computed styles for the Tailwind v4 migration (#513), not from a report. `globals.css`'s base layer read `var(--color-border)`, `var(--color-background)` and `var(--color-foreground)`, which are defined **only** inside the `@theme inline` block. Tailwind v3 (installed: 3.4.19) ignores that block, so all three declarations were invalid at computed-value time. They still beat preflight's `border-color` in the cascade and fell back to the initial value, **`currentColor`**. Measured in Chromium on the real `Card` class string: `oklch(0.145 0 0)` light / `oklch(0.985 0 0)` dark on `main`, vs `oklch(0.922 0 0)` / `oklch(1 0 0 / 0.1)` after. A sweep found **38** shipped class strings with a border width and no colour, including `Card`, `Dialog`, `AlertDialog`, `Alert`, `DropdownMenu` and `Select`, so this had become the app's de facto look. `<body>` escaped only because `layout.tsx` repeats `bg-background text-foreground`. The base layer now reads the raw tokens, which is also the right shape for #513: under v4 a theme alias is computed on `:root` and would carry the light value into `.dark`. `theme-vars-defined.test.ts` fails on any `var(--x)` outside `@theme` with no declaration outside `@theme`, so it covers the defect class rather than these three lines. Mutation-checked: restoring either alias fails it, and disabling the `@theme` strip trips the vacuity assertions. **Known consequence**: the hand-rolled `<input>` in `DraftGenerator.tsx` drops to the same 1.26:1 boundary the shared `Input` already has. That is a system-wide 1.4.11 question, not a new one.

### 2026-09-12

- **Changing the default export format in Settings did nothing until a reload (#693, P3.39)**: found while examining `ExportOptionsModal`'s `set-state-in-effect` site for #584 — and it is **not a lint problem**: fixing one would not have fixed the other, which is why it was filed and fixed separately. `optionsTouchedRef` guarded *every* preference arrival, is **never reset**, and this component **never unmounts** — `books/[bookId]/page.tsx:606` renders it unconditionally rather than behind `{showExportModal && …}`, with visibility carried by the `isOpen` prop. So one touch of the format control disabled the stored defaults for the life of the page, and the comment's *"until they interact"* meant per-**mount** rather than per-open. **#674 is what made it reachable**: giving `useUserPreferences` real subscribers meant a Settings save now notifies mounted consumers, where before it was only ever seen by the next component to mount — so the bug was inert until the fix that exposed it. The ref now guards the **first** arrival only. Preferences load asynchronously, so a format picked in the moment before they land must still not be overwritten; a *later* change can only come from the user deliberately saving new preferences, and that wins over a stale ad-hoc choice. Reopening the modal keeps the last choice, because nothing resets it — stated rather than left implicit, since the alternative (reset on close) would re-pick the saved default on every open and annoy repeat exporters. **A mutation found an unreachable guard in my own fix**: the first cut also compared the preferences object's identity, and dropping that comparison passed all four tests — because the effect's `[userPreferences]` dependency already does exactly that job. Removed rather than justified, which is #672's rule applied to my own code. Four tests, and `main`'s component fails the one that matters. Four mutations: dropping the first-arrival guard, restoring the old guard-everything behaviour, never applying preferences, and the redundant identity check that is now gone.

- **The summary page's button said "Saving..." while it was loading, and that is why the lint fix had to wait (#584, P3.31)**: the follow-up #691 named rather than shipped. `isLoading` was **one flag doing two jobs** — the fetch effect wrote it, the submit handler wrote it, and its only reader was the submit button, whose label is `isLoading ? 'Saving...' : 'Continue to TOC Generation'`. So every visit to the page showed **"Saving..." while the summary was being fetched**, before anything had been saved. Not latent: the effect sets it on every mount. **Proved rather than asserted** — `main`'s component fails two of the four new tests, including "does not say Saving... while it is loading". Split into `summaryLoaded` (the raw fact the effect learns) and `isSaving` (the submit path), with **`isLoadingSummary` derived** from the first: `Boolean(bookId) && !summaryLoaded`. Deriving rather than storing is what covers the no-book case without an effect announcing it, and it removes the synchronous `setIsLoading(true)` that #584 flagged — `set-state-in-effect` 16 → 15, with the file's ledger row lowered 2 → 1 rather than pruned, since its `localStorage` restore is still hydration-constrained. The page had **no test file**; it now has four tests pinning what the button says and when, plus the `[bookId]`-change case where the app router keeps the component mounted and the cleanup has to re-arm the state. **One mutation exposed a test that could not discriminate**: `isLoadingSummary = !summaryLoaded` — dropping the `bookId` guard — passed the no-book test, because "disabled" reads the same whether the reason is an empty summary or a fetch that will never run. Typing a ready summary and asserting the button *enables* separates them, and the mutation then fails. Four mutations in total, each with its substitution asserted.

- **One more `set-state-in-effect` site, and one fix reverted before it shipped a regression (#584, P3.31)**: took a per-site pass over all 17 remaining sites against the current tree rather than trusting the earlier classification. Two looked mechanically fixable and were probed with `npx eslint` on the single file rather than reasoned about; **one was, and one was a trap.** **`ClarifyingQuestions.tsx:54` is fixed**: the effect ended `else { setIsLoadingResponses(false) }`, announcing "there is nothing to load" — a fact already knowable from `bookId` and `questions` during render — by writing it into the *fetch's* state. The state now means one thing, *the fetch has not finished*, and the skeleton reads both conditions where it is rendered. 17 → 16. **`summary/page.tsx:45` is reverted, and the reason is the useful part**: initialising `isLoading` to `true` and dropping the synchronous `setIsLoading(true)` does clear the warning — verified — but `isLoading` is **one flag doing two jobs**, and the second is the submit button's label. The page would have opened with its button reading **"Saving..."** before anything was saved. Caught by reading the other `setIsLoading` call sites before writing the test, not by a test. The real fix is to split the flag into `isLoadingSummary` and `isSaving`, which is a behaviour-affecting refactor of a page with no test file — worth doing, and not as a quiet rider on a lint fix. Recorded on #584 so the next pass starts from the bounded change rather than rediscovering the trap. Three tests on the fix, and the mutation table is the interesting part: hard-wiring the derivation to `false` fails, dropping its `questions.length` condition fails, and **restoring the deleted `else` branch passes** — which is the point, not a gap. The branch was redundant rather than load-bearing, and the old code passing the new tests is how that is known. 151 suites / 2705 passed.

- **Ten of the eleven duplicate changelog sections merged by deleting a line, and the convention decided (#661, P3.43)**: #661's last two acceptance criteria. **The guard's own premise was wrong, and checking it made the work trivial.** `test_changelog_headings.py` ledgered eleven duplicated dates with the reason that merging them meant "reordering historical entries … a large, hard-to-review diff over text whose within-day order may carry meaning". That is true of **one** of them. In the other ten, every section of a given date was **contiguous** — so merging was deleting the redundant heading line and nothing else: **`0 insertions, 20 deletions`**, no content moved, no entry reordered, no judgement exercised about order. `2026-07-18` really is the hard case — its four sections are split by five other dates — so it stays ledgered with that as the reason rather than the over-generalised one. The stale-row rule then fired on the other ten, as designed. **The convention question is answered rather than left open**: #661 offered appending-within-day or one-file-per-entry, and both are rejected now that the union merge driver removed the cost they existed to remove. Appending buries the newest entry of a busy day at the bottom; file-per-entry adds a build step and makes the changelog generated rather than edited. Keeping the shape people already write and fixing the merge is the smaller change, and `docs/references/documentation-management.md` now records that — the convention, the driver, the duplicate-heading rule, the deliberate `### 2026-07-21 (evening)` suffix form, and the one remaining ledgered date. Three mutations: a duplicate heading fails, re-ledgering a merged date fails as stale, and removing the `merge=union` line fails the reproduction guard.

- **The destructive red failed AA in every state, and `text-destructive` was failing on 44 sites nobody had ticketed (#682, P3.36)**: #682 was filed about the *button* — white on `rgb(239, 68, 68)` is **3.76:1**, below AA in every state including enabled, on two irreversible actions ("Delete Book", "Delete account permanently"). Measuring the fix turned up that the same value drove `text-destructive`, and **that role was failing too**: 3.76:1 on a light background, 3.45:1 on `bg-muted`, across 44 call sites the issue never mentioned. **The two uses pull opposite ways on a dark background** — white needs a dark red to sit on, red text needs a light one — and no single value does both: red-700 is 6.47:1 under white and 3.06:1 on `--background`; red-400 is 7.16:1 there and leaves white at 2.24:1. So the role is now two colours: `destructive.DEFAULT` follows the theme (red-700 light, red-400 dark) and `destructive.surface` is theme-fixed red-700 for the five opaque fills. Every surface measured — light 6.47 / 6.47 / 5.93 on background / card / muted and **5.45** on the `bg-destructive/10` error card; dark 7.16 / 6.48 / 5.47 and **4.76** on `bg-destructive/20`. `dark:bg-destructive/60` came off the Button and Badge variants: against a dark-red base it composites to a near-black control that passes contrast at 11.37 while no longer reading as red. **Two rounds of pre-PR review, and each found something the guards could not.** The first cut did it with a `.dark .text-destructive` stylesheet override; the reviewer found that `text-destructive/90`, `data-[variant=destructive]:text-destructive`, `data-[variant=destructive]:focus:text-destructive`, `data-[error=true]:text-destructive` and `*:data-[slot=alert-description]:text-destructive/90` are each a **different class** the override cannot reach — the #632 trap, five times over. Moving to a token removed that surface entirely. **The second finding is the one worth reading**: a theme-aware `var(--destructive)` measures perfectly and makes Tailwind v3 stop emitting **every** opacity variant. Built the CSS to check rather than take it on trust: `bg-destructive/10`, `border-destructive/20`, `text-destructive/90` and `ring-destructive/20` each emitted **zero rules**. Error-card tints, invalid rings and destructive menu backgrounds would have shipped colourless with the whole theme suite green, because every guard in that directory reads the config and does colour maths and **none of them can see whether a class produces a rule**. Fixed with the channel form `rgb(var(--destructive-rgb) / <alpha-value>)`, and `destructive-utilities-emitted.test.ts` now **builds the stylesheet** and asserts each of the 15 destructive classes in the tree appears in it — reverting to the plain `var()` fails 9 tests. **Two existing ledgers caught the knock-ons before the suite did**: #635's spinner ledger named `destructive` as `DeleteBookModal`'s button surface, which is now `destructive-surface`, and its resolver read oklch only so the new rgb token priced as null. #642's recorded shortfall flips from "there is a gap, and it is this big" to "there is no gap", which is what recording it was for. Five mutations on top of that, each with its substitution asserted. **This is a visible brand change**, a deeper red in light and a lighter one in dark; reverting is `tailwind.config.js` plus two lines of `globals.css`, and the guards state the ratios they expect, so a revert fails loudly. 151 suites / 2702 passed, 0 eslint errors.

- **The staging E2E account is not "a normal, fully-privileged staging user" — it is more privileged, in the dimension that costs money (#603, P3.32)**: #603 reasons from the premise that a future leak of that credential has the same blast radius as #599's did, and lists "accepting the risk, on the grounds that staging holds nothing real" as a legitimate answer. The premise is wrong, and the option is weaker than it looks. The account's email is written to the backend as `E2E_EXEMPT_EMAILS`, and `_is_exempt_e2e_user` is consulted at **three** places in `dependencies.py`: the per-user rate limiter (#180), the AI generation quota (#173), and plan entitlement checks. So a leaked credential does not buy an ordinary account's access — it buys **unmetered AI generation against the operator's provider budget**, and paid-tier features without a plan. Staging's *data* is disposable; its *spend* is not, and that is a different argument from the one the issue is weighing. **Checked rather than assumed in the other direction too**: the production fence is real and is tested — `_is_exempt_e2e_user` returns `False` whenever `is_production_env()` is true, and `test_never_exempt_in_production` asserts it, with two further tests pinning that an exempt user skips the quota and a non-exempt one is still capped. So there is no gap to close there, which is worth stating because it would have been an easy guard to write and a waste. Recorded in `frontend/tests/e2e/staging/README.md` — the file #603's first acceptance criterion names as the home for the decision — as **facts, not a decision**: the scoping choice is the owner's, and the section says so explicitly rather than quietly making it. The other three criteria still need the staging box: deleting the five `+aa550-*` throwaway accounts from a live Atlas cluster, and updating the demo doc to match whatever happens.

- **A PR opened as a draft was never reviewed, and a draft that was pushed to burned a full one (#607, P3.34)**: `glm-review.yml` triggered on `[opened, synchronize]` with no draft guard, and the two defaults were wrong in **opposite** directions — `opened` fires for drafts, so a sizeable draft consumed a 35-minute / 60-turn review of work explicitly not ready; and because `ready_for_review` was not a trigger, a PR opened as a draft and later marked ready got **no review at all** unless something else happened to push to it. Fixing either alone makes the other worse: skipping drafts without adding `ready_for_review` turns "wasteful" into "never reviewed". One decision, both halves: **review exactly once, when the PR is ready for people to read.** Guarded, because this failure leaves no mark — a workflow that does not run produces no red check, so a regression shows up as reviews quietly not happening. `scripts/test_glm_review_draft_policy.py` asserts both halves **and the three guards that predate them** (#270's fork guard, #519's bot-author guard, #605's `sender.type` guard), since they share one long `if:` and are easy to drop while editing it. Five mutations, each with its substitution count asserted after the lesson from #684: removing `ready_for_review`, removing the draft guard, and dropping any of the older clauses each fail. **The issue's other three items were re-checked rather than assumed**, and all three are still true: branch protection still requires only `Frontend Tests`, `Backend Tests`, `E2E Tests (Playwright)` and `Security Audit`, so GLM Review can skip freely and the "promoting it would strand bot PRs" trap stays a note; `upload_transcript` still defaults to `true` at the pinned callee SHA and the repo is still public, which is the same category as #599 with a different answer; and item 1 still needs events this session cannot manufacture — a Dependabot PR clearing the 20-addition gate, and a real large-diff review that times out.

- **Two ledger reasons pointed at an issue that was about to close without answering them (#684, P3.38)**: `stranded-accent-baseline.json` carried two forward pointers to #642 — *"Tracked for legibility in #642"* on `TocReview.tsx`'s accept button, and *"whether disabled states are legible enough in practice is #642"* on `ClarifyingQuestions.tsx`. #683 closed #642 by fixing the shared `<Button>` component, which reaches neither: both rows are raw `<button>` elements that `buttonVariants` never styled. A ledger row whose reason cites a closed issue is the stale-row problem one level up — the row still passes, and the reason has quietly stopped being true. **They are different cases and get different answers.** `TocReview`'s is a **busy** button — disabled *because it is saving*, and saying so — so the inactive-component exemption is weak for it and it is repointed to #684, which measured it at **4.09:1** along with three worse siblings. `ClarifyingQuestions`' two are genuinely unavailable (Previous with no earlier question, Next with nothing answered), so #642's decision *is* the answer: option 1 deliberately left the unavailable state alone, the exemption stands, and the row now records that rather than an open question. Found by sweeping every issue number in the guards and ledgers against its GitHub state — 52 references, and these were the only two forward pointers to a closing issue. The rest cite the issue that created the guard, which is history and correct.
- **The four raw `<button>` busy labels #683 could not reach (#684, P3.38)**: #642's fix landed on the shared `<Button>`, and six busy labels sit on hand-styled raw `<button>`s that `buttonVariants` never touched — so they neither had the defect nor got the fix. Measured rather than assumed: `edit-toc` and `summary` at **2.29** light (indigo-600 under `disabled:opacity-50`), `NotReadyMessage` at **2.35**, `TocReview`'s accept at **4.09** (green-400 on green-800, no opacity involved), and two that already pass — `TocReview`'s regenerate at 4.61/5.86 and `summary`'s microphone toggle at 4.83. **The fixes are smaller than the fix for `<Button>` was, because the shapes differ.** Three of the four are disabled *only* while busy, so their disabled styling **is** their busy styling and the treatment simply comes off: dropping `disabled:text-green-400` puts `TocReview`'s label at **7.13**, and dropping `disabled:opacity-50` does the same for `NotReadyMessage`. The other two are disabled for **two different reasons** — saving, and an empty TOC or an invalid summary — so the opacity became conditional, full while saving (**6.29**) and greyed while genuinely unavailable, where 1.4.3 exempts it. `aria-busy` everywhere it belongs; the microphone toggle is exempt with the reason written down, since `aria-pressed` already says what it is and "this element is being updated" would be the wrong word. **The #637 guard caught the consequence before the suite did**: removing `text-green-400` made `stranded-accent-baseline.json`'s `TocReview` row stale, which failed the build until the row was pruned — the third time today a stale-row rule has fired on something real rather than a planted mutation. The sweep now covers raw `<button>` too, with the same floor so narrowing the label pattern fails rather than checking less. **A mutation that did not apply looked exactly like one that survived**: the `aria-busy` removal for `NotReadyMessage` used fourteen spaces of indentation where the file has ten, so it matched nothing and the suite passed. One keystroke from being written up as a gap in the guard. Every mutation now asserts its own substitution count.

- **Busy buttons rendered their own status text at 2.29:1 — option 1, with the fork decided (#642, P3.42)**: `button.tsx` puts `disabled:opacity-50` in the shared base string, and CSS `opacity` groups the element, so the fill and the label are **each** blended 50% with the page behind the button rather than composited against each other. For a button that is merely unavailable that is right — WCAG 1.4.3 exempts inactive components and the greyed look is the affordance — but this codebase also uses `disabled` for **busy**, on buttons that render their own status text while working. The default variant's label falls from 6.29:1 to **2.29:1** in light: the hardest text on the screen to read, at the moment it is the only signal the action is running. #642 offered three options and asked for a decision. **Option 1 is taken**, because it is the only one that reaches AA and because it changes nothing about the unavailable state: a `busy` prop restores full opacity, sets `aria-busy`, keeps the button unclickable, and is opt-in, so no existing call site moves unless it says so. Default-variant busy labels go **2.29 → 6.29**. **The issue's call-site list was incomplete, and so was my first sweep — which is the point of the second half of the guard**: the issue named five, an enumerated-verb pattern found **eleven**, and the pre-PR reviewer found that pattern missing more (`Verifying…`, `Changing…`, `Please wait…`). Enumerating verbs taken from what the sweep had already found is circular; the real shape is a gerund plus an ellipsis. Broadened to `/(\b\w+ing|Please wait)(\.\.\.|…)/`, it found **twelve more** — `reset-password`, `verify-2fa`, `profile`, `QuestionContainer`, `DataRecoveryModal`, `ActiveSessionsList`, `BillingSettingsForm` ×2, `PasswordChangeForm`, `TwoFactorSetup` ×2 and another example — for **23** in all, more than double. **And narrowing the pattern could not fail the sweep**, because a sweep only fails on what it matches: a floor assertion (`>= 23` busy-labelled Buttons found) now makes that mutation fail, which is the thing that had been missing. The sites `<Button>`s whose label turns into a busy string while they disable themselves — `forgot-password`, `settings`, `profile`, `BookCreationWizard`, `DeleteBookModal`, `ChapterEditor`, `DraftGenerationButton`, `RegenerateQuestionsDialog`, `ProfilePictureUpload` and both examples. Two carry compound conditions (`!isConfirmed || isDeleting`), where `busy` takes the *busy* half only, so a button greyed for being unconfirmed still looks unavailable. A further set of busy labels sit on **raw `<button>` elements** with their own styling, which this fix does not reach and which `buttonVariants` never touched either — named here rather than left as a silent gap. **Guarded in two halves**: the ratios are measured from `tailwind.config.js` and `globals.css` (including a test that *reproduces the defect* at the base opacity, so the fix is measured against a failure that still exists), and a brace-balanced JSX sweep fails any `<Button>` with a busy label and no `busy` — the parser pinned to fixtures including `disabled={count > 0}`, which a naive `indexOf('>')` mis-slices. **The reviewer's second finding was a hole in the feature itself**: every class in `BUSY_CLASSES` is `disabled:`-prefixed, and `asChild` renders someone else's element with no `disabled` attribute — so none of them applied and a "busy" link stayed fully clickable, the opposite of what the prop advertises. Reproduced before fixing (the probe printed `pointer-events-none: false`), then fixed with an unprefixed class set plus `aria-disabled`, since an anchor cannot carry `disabled`. Eleven mutations: dropping `busy` from a call site, reverting `BUSY_CLASSES` to `opacity-50`, setting the base opacity to 100, a low-contrast brand fill, removing `aria-busy`, removing the implied `disabled`, passing `disabled` through `asChild`, applying the busy classes unconditionally, reverting the `asChild` class set, dropping `aria-disabled`, and narrowing the label pattern. **One thing this does not fix, filed as #682**: the `destructive` variant's white label is **3.76:1** on the brand red in *every* state, enabled included — `busy` takes the two delete buttons from 1.97 to 3.76 and no further. That shortfall is the red itself, a brand decision, and the guard pins the current figure so whoever changes it sees this test fail and finds the issue. 149 suites / 2675 passed, 0 eslint errors.

- **The TypeScript 7 blocker was an unused dependency — and removing it revealed a harder one the issue never named (#514, P3.25)**: #514 records `ts-jest`'s `typescript >=4.3 <7` peer as what caps TypeScript at 5. **`ts-jest` is not used.** It appears in exactly one place in the whole tree — its own line in `devDependencies`. Jest runs through `next/jest`, whose transform resolves to `next/dist/build/swc/jest-transformer.js`; nothing imports `ts-jest`, no config names it, and the lockfile lists **no** package depending on it. It is also the **only** package in the tree peering `typescript <7` — `eslint-config-next` peers `>=3.3.1`. Removed, and with it seven transitive packages that nothing else needed, `handlebars` and `uglify-js` among them, which shrinks the supply-chain surface as a side effect. 147 suites / 2661 passed, 0 eslint errors, every gate green, clean-room `npm ci` with CI's npm. **Then the measurement the issue wanted**: on a throwaway bump to `typescript@7.0.2`, `tsc --noEmit` reports **37 errors**, all `TS2304: Cannot find name 'jest'`, in five `__mocks__` files — TS 7 no longer picks up `@types/jest`'s globals, and an explicit `"types": ["node", "jest"]` does not restore them. Importing `{ jest } from '@jest/globals'` does fix those five files, and converts them into **27 `TS2345`** errors instead, because `@jest/globals`' `jest.fn()` infers `never` where the ambient global did not — so the mocks need explicit generics, which is real work with behavioural risk. **And the blocker that actually decides it**: `npx eslint` under TS 7 does not lint at all — *"typescript-eslint does not support TS 7.0"*, tracked upstream at typescript-eslint#10940 for **TS >= 7.1**. `npm run lint` is a required check, so the upgrade is gated on typescript-eslint regardless of anything in this repo. #514 is re-scoped accordingly: the ts-jest half is done, and what remains is upstream plus a measured 27-error mock migration.

- **A commit message that said it would not close an issue closed it (#584, P3.31)**: #676's merge commit carried the sentence *"Does not close #584 — its condition is the rules back at `error`"*, written specifically to prevent a closure. It closed #584, with **17 violations outstanding and all three rules still at `warn`**. GitHub's linked-issue parser matches `close|fixes|resolved #<n>` anywhere in a commit message or PR body and does not parse negation, so the disclaimer was the trigger. Reopened, with the state wrong for the fourteen hours in between and nothing else lost. `scripts/check-commit-message.sh` now runs at the `commit-msg` stage and rejects a negated closing keyword, pinned by fourteen fixtures on **both** sides — the six shapes it must reject (including the real sentence, verbatim) and the seven ordinary messages it must leave alone, deliberate `Closes #584` among them, because a guard that rejects everything is as useless as one that rejects nothing and only the second half of that is obvious. Its limitation is stated rather than hidden: a PR **body** closes issues the same way and no local hook can read it. **Two defects in the guard itself, both found by exercising it rather than reading it.** The wiring assertion was vacuous: "is this hook wired up" checked for the string `commit-msg` anywhere in the pre-commit config, which also matches `default_install_hook_types` a hundred lines away, so retagging the hook to `stages: [manual]` left it green — it now parses the YAML and asserts the hook's own `stages`. And it skipped lines beginning with `#`, reasoning that git strips comments: git does, in **editor** mode, while `git commit -m` uses `--cleanup=whitespace` and keeps them. The very first message this guard ran on opened a body line with an issue number, and the skip discarded that line along with the negated keyword inside it — the guard passed and the message would have closed the issue a second time. Nothing is stripped now: a comment line that trips it is a reword, a missed closure is the failure that already cost us. **A third came from the pre-PR review and was the same class again**: `grep` is line-based, commit bodies wrap at ~72 columns, so *"This does not"* / *"close #123"* across two lines read as safe while GitHub, which does not care about the wrap, closes the issue. Reproduced against the previous version before fixing — newlines now collapse to spaces before matching. All three defects were in the *filtering* of the input rather than in the pattern, which is the shape to look for in any guard that reads a file line by line. Eight mutations pass, including reverting each of the three fixes individually. **The guard for the guard was itself vacuous, and mutation is what found it**: the "is this hook actually wired up" assertion read the config as text and checked `"commit-msg" in config` — a string that also appears in `default_install_hook_types` a hundred lines away, so retagging the hook to `stages: [manual]` left it green. It now parses the YAML and asserts `hooks[0]["stages"] == ["commit-msg"]`. Six mutations pass: retagging the stage, dropping `commit-msg` from the install types, deleting the hook block, removing the negation requirement (which then rejects legitimate closures), stubbing the check to always pass, and making the script non-executable. **A fourth instance of a known mistake, recorded**: the mutation loop's `git checkout HEAD -- .pre-commit-config.yaml` silently deleted the uncommitted hook block it was testing, and the next run read like a broken test. The standing lesson is to commit before mutating; the gap was applying it to the file under mutation and not to the other files the same loop reverts.

- **Eight changelog conflicts in one session, fixed without touching how entries are written (#661, P3.43)**: since #661 was filed, **every** PR has conflicted on `docs/CHANGELOG.md` and nothing else — #673 against #671, #670 against #673, #674 against both, #676 against #674, #677 against #676. All positional, all mechanical, none semantic; two landed on top of another conflict in the same file minutes later. #661 deliberately leaves the *convention* question (append-within-day vs. file-per-entry) to a human because it changes how everyone writes entries. **This is not that.** One line in `.gitattributes` — `docs/CHANGELOG.md merge=union` — makes git keep both sides on a conflicting hunk, which for an append-only log where every PR inserts a distinct line at the same offset is exactly the resolution a human performs by hand, every time. The convention is untouched and the question stays open; it just stops costing a resolution per PR while it waits. **Guarded by reproduction, not by grep.** `scripts/test_changelog_merge.py` builds a throwaway repo, copies **this repo's real `.gitattributes`** into it, and runs the actual case: two branches each inserting an entry under the same `### <date>`. Checking that a line exists in a config file would prove nothing about whether git honours it. The anti-vacuity half builds the same fixture *without* the driver and asserts it still conflicts — a fixture that cannot conflict would make the other assertion pass whatever `.gitattributes` said. It also pins the thing that matters after the merge: both entries present, **one** heading, no markers. Two mutations: deleting the line fails it, and swapping `union` for a driver that does not exist fails it too (git falls back silently, which is the likelier real-world breakage). Wired in already — CI's `pytest scripts/ -q` picks it up, 83 passed. Known limits, stated on the issue: the blank line between two merged entries is collapsed (still one markdown list, renders correctly), and whether GitHub's server-side mergeability check honours `.gitattributes` is untested — every one of the eight above was paid in a local rebase, which does honour it.
- **The nwsapi pin's root cause, found and reduced to two lines (#589, P2.26)**: #589 pinned `nwsapi` to `2.2.25` because `2.2.26`+ turned a 23-test keyboard file into 16 timeouts and ~220s, and attributed it to that release's resolver-cache rewrite. The cache was innocent. **Reproduced standalone** — no React, no jest, no testing-library, just `jsdom@26.1.0` and a document containing one `div`: `matches(':modal')` costs **~500ms per call** on 2.2.26/2.2.27 against **0.01–0.15ms** on 2.2.25. **It does not scale with DOM depth** (5 levels and 25 levels cost the same) **but scales linearly with JS stack size** — `--stack-size=200` → 17ms, default → 368ms, `--stack-size=2000` → 1569ms. That is recursion ending in a stack overflow, not tree traversal. The two lines: 2.2.26's new `matchesNative()` resolves `_matches || node.matches || …`, and `_matches` is assigned **only** inside `install()`, which the source itself marks "only for browsers". jsdom never calls `install()` — it calls nwsapi's `match()` from its own `Element.prototype.matches` — so `_matches` is `undefined`, the chain falls through to `node.matches`, and **`node.matches` is nwsapi**. Every `:modal` re-enters nwsapi, reaches `isModal`, and calls itself until `RangeError`, which `catch (e) { return false; }` swallows. The answer comes back correct, nothing is logged, and the only symptom is the time. It also corrects the profile's reading: the 29,999,829 `:fullscreen` calls are not a thousand nested evaluations per `:modal`, they are one several-thousand-frame recursion chain per call. **Nothing operational changes** — the override stays and `ChapterTab.keyboard.test.tsx` remains the tripwire — but the close condition is now specific rather than "wait for a release". The upstream report is drafted verbatim in `claudedocs/nwsapi-2.2.26-modal-recursion.md`, with the repro, both scaling tables, the cause and three candidate fixes, ready to post with nothing to rewrite. **Deliberately not filed**: publishing an issue on a third party's repository under the owner's identity is theirs to do, not mine.

- **One more `set-state-in-effect` site, and the route I had said did not exist (#584, P3.31)**: I wrote on #584 that `ExportProgressModal:72` had *"no fix I could find that does not either keep the synchronous reset or read a ref during render"*. It had one, and #674 found it by using it elsewhere: **reset in the effect's cleanup**. `elapsedTime` is only read while the status is `processing` or `pending`, so clearing it on the way out instead of on the way in is invisible — and it costs one render pass fewer. The component **had no test file at all**, so nothing covered the counter, the 10s and 20s thresholds it drives, or what a second export sees. Five tests now do, and the pair that matters cuts both ways: dropping the cleanup reset fails two of them, while **the original pre-refactor code passes all five** — which is how the change is known to preserve behaviour rather than merely to be green. 18 → 17, and the gate from #675 demanded its own ledger row be pruned on its first real use, one PR after landing. **A change that did *not* ship, and the reason matters more than the one that did**: I had also called `ActiveSessionsList:61` "exact" — hoist `setLoadState('loading')` into the Retry handler, since `loadState` already initialises to `'loading'` and Retry is the only other caller. Made it, measured it, and the warning stayed: `set-state-in-effect` flags **any** `setState` reachable inside an `async` callee, `await`-first or not, so the shape of the reset was never the objection. Reverted. Probing that boundary found what the rule can see: converting the callee to a `.then()` chain, changing nothing else, **silences it**. That makes every remaining fetch kick-off mechanically "fixable" by reshaping readable `async`/`await` into promise chains to suit a linter that cannot analyse `await` — which would close #584 and leave the code worse. Recorded on the issue as a trap rather than a route, and it is the clearest argument for the ledger: the 17 cannot grow, and each can be fixed when there is a reason beyond the count.

- **`set-state-in-effect` was the one backlog in this repo with no ledger (#675, P3.35)**: `eslint.config.mjs` holds three `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` 7 rules at `warn` so the ESLint 9 upgrade could land as an upgrade, and #584 tracks burning them to zero. The remainder will not go quickly — the per-site classification shows fetch kick-offs needing a data-layer decision, a latch, and initialization-from-storage constrained by hydration. Meanwhile `npx eslint .` prints **437 warnings**, so a new violation lands in that pile and nobody sees it. The backlog was never the risk; the backlog growing invisibly was. Every other backlog here is already a ledger that can only shrink — `security-baseline.json`, `gray-literal-baseline.json`, `dark-overlay-baseline.json`, `stranded-accent-baseline.json`, `typecheck-test-baseline.json`, `unrun-spec-baseline.json` — and this one had a downgraded rule instead. `scripts/react-hooks-gate.mjs` + `react-hooks-baseline.json` give it the same shape: counts **per file per rule** (a per-file total would let a `refs` violation swap in for a `set-state-in-effect` one and net out, the #624 rule), a **stale row fails** rather than silently pre-authorising the next violation on that file (#631), and a count that drops is **reported, never failed** — `audit_gate.py`'s contract, since nobody's build should break because someone fixed something. Wired into `Frontend Tests` (7.7s). **The gate proved itself twice against the live tree before it ever ran in CI**: the baseline was first generated before #670 merged, so it ledgered four `refs` rows that were already fixed — flagged as stale, exactly the case it exists for. Then rebasing past #674 left two more (`LoadingStateManager`, `useUserPreferences`), flagged the same way. Neither was a hypothetical mutation; both were the ordinary consequence of a ledger written at one commit and read at another, which is how a stale row gets into a repo in the first place. **Seven mutations, covering both directions and the ways the gate itself could go quiet**: a new violation in a ledgered file fails (`1 -> 2`); one in an unledgered file fails; deleting a row while the violation remains fails; a row whose violation is gone fails as stale; an inflated count is reported and still exits 0; pointing the sweep at a 3-file subtree fails the vacuity assertion instead of passing over nearly nothing; and non-JSON output fails as a broken run rather than parsing to an empty set. This does **not** close #584 — its condition is the rules back at `error`, which stays right. It makes the interval until then safe, which is what was missing.

- **Three more of #584's `set-state-in-effect` sites, and one of them was hiding a real staleness bug (#584, P3.31)**: `useUserPreferences` kept a module-level cache and an effect that mirrored it into component state. The mirror was not merely an extra render — **an already-mounted consumer never saw an invalidation**. The settings and profile pages both call `invalidateUserPreferencesCache(updated)` after a save, but a consumer whose effect had already run had nothing to re-run it, so the editor, draft generator, export modal and draft button kept serving the pre-save preferences until they remounted. The existing test only proved a *newly rendered* hook picked the new value up, which is the one case that was never broken. The cache is now a real external store — a listener set, `publish()` on fill and on invalidation, and `useSyncExternalStore` with `getServerSnapshot: () => null` to match the old first paint. A consumer mounting mid-fetch still gets the result (it subscribes instead of chaining its own `.then` onto the shared promise), and `inflight` is only cleared by the generation that set it, so a stale fetch settling cannot null out a newer one. **`LoadingStateManager`'s two mirrors are genuine derivations**: `visible` is `isLoading && delayElapsed` and `timeRemaining` is `estimatedTime - countedDown`, both computed during render, leaving the effects owning only the timers. Because `isLoading` alone already forces `visible` false, the reset moved into effect cleanup and a finished load no longer costs a second render pass. **Mutation checking found that refactor untested in my own code**: dropping either cleanup reset left all 53 tests green, because not one of them loads twice — so a second load would have skipped the 200ms anti-flicker delay entirely, and the countdown would have carried over and told a user that a fresh 5-second operation had 2 seconds left. Two tests now cover the second load, and each mutation fails exactly one. The invalidation fix is likewise pinned by a test that fails against the old effect-mirror hook. `set-state-in-effect` 21 → 18 (`refs` unchanged at 4); the rules stay at `warn` until the count reaches zero, per #584's close condition. 146 suites / 2656 passed, 0 eslint errors, typecheck clean.

- **`react-hooks/refs` back to `error` — the rule I had called un-investigable (#584, P3.31)**: I had recorded that `BookCreationWizard.tsx:100` was flagged at `form.handleSubmit(onSubmit)` for reasons I could not explain, and that fixing a submit path with an in-flight guard on a guess is how double-submits ship — then treated the whole rule as blocked on that. **It was blocked on reading the rule, which sits in `node_modules`.** The implementation makes it plain: on a `CallExpression`/`MethodCall` evaluated *during render*, if any argument is a function carrying `readRefEffect`, it errors — because the callee might invoke it during render. `handleSubmit` is an opaque library call and `onSubmit` reads `submitInFlight.current`. So the fix is to move the *call* out of render: `onSubmit={(e) => form.handleSubmit(onSubmit)(e)}`. `handleSubmit(onSubmit)` returns a submit handler, so invoking it with the event is equivalent — one line, behaviour preserved, hypothesis confirmed by measurement before it was written. The other three sites are each a different shape, as recorded: `useSwipeGesture.ts` assigned its latest-callbacks ref during render (moved into an effect — the write was safe, since `latest.current` is only read in event handlers, but a render-phase ref write is exactly what the rule exists to catch); and `dashboard/page.tsx` read `hasLoadedOnce.current` during render to choose the skeleton **while also** reading it in a callback where a ref is correct, so it now carries both a ref for the callbacks and state for the render, written together so they cannot drift. **Mutation-checked three ways, and the fourth attempt caught a faulty mutation rather than a passing guard**: the dashboard mutation initially reported `errorCount: 0` because shell escaping had mangled `&&` and the edit never applied — the exact near-miss flagged hours earlier, where a mutation that did not run is indistinguishable from a rule that does not fire. Redone without shell interpolation it gives `errorCount: 2`. `react-hooks/refs` tree-wide: **0**, and its `"warn"` downgrade deleted. With #669 this is two of #584's three rules enforced; `set-state-in-effect` (26 sites) remains, and is genuinely a React refactor rather than a reading problem.

- **TabBar forked production code on a test environment, and the branch was already dead (#672, P3.44)**: `TabBar` checked `typeof activeTab.scrollIntoView === 'function'` and, in the `else`, set `canScrollUp`/`canScrollDown` to `true` unconditionally — "for test environments" — where `handleScroll` derives both from `scrollTop`. Two writers for one pair of state values, one of them lying. Surfaced while classifying #584's `set-state-in-effect` sites; flagged there, but not a state-derivation problem. **The issue I filed for it was wrong within five minutes**: `jest.setup.ts:134` already stubs `Element.prototype.scrollIntoView` (added for Radix Select), so the `else` cannot run even under jest — the premise "the real `scrollIntoView` path is never exercised" was false, and this is dead-code removal, not a coverage fix. **My first probe was also wrong, in a way worth recording**: a `throw` in the `else` staying silent across 36 tests does not distinguish *the branch is dead* from *the whole effect body is skipped*, and in `TabBar.test.tsx` it is the latter — that suite stubs `ChapterTab` to a `div` that drops `...provided.draggableProps`, so the `querySelector` returns `null` and the effect returns before either branch. Silence there was guaranteed either way. Re-probed at the branch point across all six suites that mount `TabBar`: **22 × `null`, 5 × reached, 0 × early return** — so the branch point *is* reached, by the suites rendering the real `ChapterTab`, and the `else` still never ran. Instrumenting the call gives the outcome rather than the absence: 5 invocations, each on the element whose `data-rfd-draggable-id` matches the active chapter, each with `{behavior:'smooth', block:'nearest'}`. The `try/catch` is kept, comment corrected — it claimed to guard a missing method in tests, when what it can catch is an engine throwing on the options object. `canScrollUp`/`canScrollDown` now have exactly one writer, which also drops one `set-state-in-effect` site. A sweep for other shipped components forking on the test environment found **none**; the three hits are benign, and `src/lib/navigation.ts` is the contrast worth naming — it exists because `window.location` cannot be stubbed under jsdom 26, but solves that with a **seam** whose runtime behaviour is byte-identical, not a branch that behaves differently when observed. 146 suites / 2652 passed, 0 eslint errors, typecheck clean.

- **Three of #584's 26 `set-state-in-effect` sites, the ones that are genuinely derivations (#584, P3.31)**: partial by design — the issue allows it, and the rule only flips back at zero. Classifying all 26 first was the useful part: they are **not one pattern**. Three are pure derivations mirrored into state by an effect, and those are fixed here. `summary/page.tsx` ran `setInputError(getSummaryReadinessError(summary))` on every keystroke — a pure function of `summary`, so it bought an extra render pass per character and a frame where the error shown lagged the text. `QuestionDisplay` did the same with `wordCount`, and removing the state also removed two places that had to reset it by hand (a cleanup and a fetch handler), which is the tell that it was never state. `use-media-query` is the interesting one: `matchMedia` is an **external store**, so `useSyncExternalStore` is the documented shape, and the old `useState(false)` + effect left one frame on the client where the value read `false` even though the query already matched — `getServerSnapshot` returns `false` so server output and first client render still agree. **The other 23 are not derivations and are deliberately untouched**: most are fetch kick-offs where the effect is correct and the rule is flagging a `setState` inside the function it calls; `VoiceTextInput`'s `currentMode` is local state seeded from a prop *and* toggled by the user, so it is a controlled/uncontrolled sync with real trade-offs rather than a derivation; and `ExportProgressModal`'s `setElapsedTime(0)` has no fix I could find that does not either keep the synchronous reset or read a ref during render — which would trip the `refs` rule enforced in the same batch. Landing those blind is how a render loop ships. **A second batch in the same PR**: the two browser-capability checks — `setVoiceSupported(...)` and `setIsSupported(...)`, both `isSpeechRecognitionSupported()` on mount — move to a shared `useSpeechRecognitionSupported()` built on `useSyncExternalStore` with a no-op subscribe, since a browser capability is a property of the environment that cannot change while the page is open. The old shape rendered `false` once on the client even where support exists, so the voice affordance flickered in on mount. **One violation was newly exposed rather than introduced**: #669's `immutability` fix moved `loadBookStats` above its effect, and the effect calling it then became visible to this rule — same behaviour, previously masked — which is why the count went 26 → 24 → 22 rather than 26 → 21. 0 eslint errors, typecheck clean, full suite green.

- **`react-hooks/immutability` back to `error`, the first of #584's three rules to close (#584, P3.31)**: the ESLint 9 migration downgraded three rules that `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` 7 makes errors by default, to keep that PR an upgrade rather than a React refactor. Re-measuring found **32 violations, not the 30 the issue recorded** (`react-hooks/refs` had grown 2→4), and — the useful part — that they are **three separate jobs whose close condition can be met per rule**, not one backlog: `immutability` is 2 mechanical sites, `refs` is 4 sites across 3 genuinely different patterns, and `set-state-in-effect` is 26 sites of real React refactoring. Closing them together means the two cheap rules stay unenforced for as long as the expensive one takes. This does the cheap one: both `immutability` violations are use-before-declare — `loadBookStats` called by an effect declared above it, `fetchProgress` called by `fetchQuestions` declared above it — where the call sits in the binding's temporal dead zone and works only because effects and async bodies run later. Declarations moved above their first use, behaviour unchanged, and the `"react-hooks/immutability": "warn"` line deleted so the rule is back at its default `error`. Mutation-checked: restoring the effect-before-declaration order yields `errorCount: 1` where it previously yielded a warning nobody reads. **The mutation also exposed a process failure worth recording**: the first attempt reported 0 errors and I nearly concluded the rule was not firing — it was my mutation that was faulty, and the file had meanwhile been reverted by a `git checkout HEAD --` that discarded the *uncommitted* fix, the fourth instance of that exact mistake in one session. Committing first made the next attempt read cleanly. 186 tests across 10 suites, typecheck clean, 0 eslint errors tree-wide.

- **Two of #599's three residual credential paths closed; the third needs a real staging run (#604, P3.33)**: **(2) the show-password invariant is now enforced.** Staging screenshots and video are still uploaded as CI artifacts on a **public** repo, safe only because the password field renders masked — and `auth/sign-in/page.tsx` has a toggle (`type={showPassword ? "text" : "password"}`). No spec clicks it, so the invariant holds; nothing enforced it, and a spec that toggled visibility (to assert the feature, or incidentally while debugging a selector) would publish the account password into every subsequent screenshot and video frame, silently and permanently. #599's README warns about this; a warning is not a control. `StagingPasswordVisibilityGate.test.ts` sweeps the staging spec tree for four shapes — the toggle by aria-label, by `getByLabel`, the `showPassword` state, and an assertion that the field's type is `text` — with fixtures pinning each pattern and a vacuity assertion on the file walk. Labelled a **tripwire, not a proof**, in #604's own terms: it catches the accidental reach for the toggle, not a determined edit that renames the label. **(3) the Locust report no longer embeds response bodies.** `staging_smoke.py` put `response.text[:300]` into two failure messages and `load-smoke-report.html` is uploaded publicly; better-auth does not echo the submitted password today, but a framework-level 422 that echoed request fields would land in a public artifact. All **eight** failure messages are now status-only — the sweep found six beyond the two the issue named — and `response.text` appears nowhere in the load suite. The status code is what diagnoses a smoke failure anyway. **(1) remains open and is explicitly not claimed**: deliberately failing an authenticated staging test and inspecting the artifact GitHub actually publishes needs a real run against the box, and it is the only evidence that catches a wrong assumption about how `upload-artifact` composes its inputs. Three mutations: a probe spec calling `getByLabel('Show password')` fails the guard naming both matching patterns, breaking the file walk trips the vacuity assertion rather than passing over an empty set, and the redaction is verified by count (`response.text` → 0) rather than by reading the diff. **A defect in the first cut of the guard, caught by its own fixture test**: the unmasked-assertion pattern's character class omitted `,`, so it missed `toHaveAttribute('type', 'text')` — the commonest shape — while passing everything else.

- **A spec no config can run reports identically to one that passes (#613, P3.37)**: the issue reported that `tests/e2e/page-objects/dashboard.page.ts` locates books by `book-item` / `books-list`, neither of which the app renders — `BookCard` renders `book-card` and the dashboard grid has no wrapper — so its helpers either time out or silently report zero. All of that is true. **Two things change the shape of the fix.** First, `booksList`, `getBookByTitle` and `getBooksCount` are **called by no spec**, so nothing was passing vacuously; the two locators that *are* used by specs (`book-${bookId}`, `book-menu`) were not in the issue's list. Second, and the reason this landed as a guard rather than a locator patch: **the live E2E suite is unaffected** — `src/e2e` (18 specs, the tree CI runs) correctly uses `book-card`, and the broken page object serves only `tests/e2e/deployment`, whose config nothing invokes. Fixing locators in specs no pipeline runs is polishing. **The guard corrected my own premise mid-investigation**: I concluded the deployment tree had no config at all, having listed only root-level `playwright*.ts`; the walk found `tests/e2e/deployment/playwright.config.ts`, so those specs are *configured* — just never *invoked*, which is a weaker and more accurate claim. What the guard did find is a genuine orphan: **`tests/e2e/ai-error-handling.spec.ts`**, 13.5KB, last touched 2025-12-22 (#67), sitting in `tests/e2e/` root, which no config's `testDir` covers — **nine months unrun**. `scripts/check-spec-coverage.mjs` walks every `*.spec.ts` under `frontend/`, resolves every `testDir` from every playwright config in the tree, and fails on any spec no config can reach, against `unrun-spec-baseline.json`. Same shape as the repo's existing "No unscanned npm lockfiles" step: a file in a place no gate looks at. It also *reports* (never fails) configs that no npm script invokes, since the deployment suite targets a live URL and is legitimately manual — "has a config" and "is ever executed" are different facts, and conflating them is what sent me down the wrong path. The orphan is ledgered rather than deleted because its subject overlaps `error-recovery-flow` and `toc-generation-resilience`, making wire-up-or-delete a coverage decision. Three mutations: a newly stranded spec fails; breaking the walk trips an explicit vacuity assertion (`< 15 specs`) rather than passing on an empty set; and a ledger row for a covered spec is reported as prunable without failing the build. Wired into `Frontend Tests`.

- **A failed refetch showed the onboarding empty state to a user with a full library (#612, P3.36)**: deleting the last book on a later page splices the list locally and steps back a page; if that step-back's own refetch then fails — a 429 is realistic, since a DELETE plus a refetch inside rapid paging reaches even the raised budget — `projects` is the spliced-empty array and `page` is 0, which rendered `EmptyBookState`: *"you haven't created any books yet"*, to someone whose library is not empty. It toasted but offered no in-place retry, so the list only came back on a window refocus or a reload. **The fix is the honest model the issue preferred**: `projects` being empty is not the same fact as "this library is empty", so a `reloadPending` flag distinguishes *emptied locally, reload pending* from *loaded and genuinely empty*, and the first renders a retry affordance instead of the onboarding state. That also gives the failed-page-change path the in-place Retry it never had. **Mutation M3 found untested code in my own fix**: removing `setReloadPending(false)` from the success path left the whole suite green, because after a successful retry `projects` is non-empty and the render never consults the flag — the staleness only shows on the *next* empty render, where it would tell a user who genuinely deleted everything that loading had failed. A fourth test now walks that sequence (fail → retry → delete the last book) and M3 fails against it. Three mutations in total, each reverted with the revert asserted: removing the render branch and removing `setReloadPending(true)` each fail both #612 tests, and removing the clear fails the recovery test. A counterpart test pins that a genuinely empty library on page 0 still gets the onboarding state, so the fix cannot swallow the case it is meant to preserve. 29 tests in `page.pagination.test.tsx`, typecheck clean.

- **Guarded the silent half of the changelog-conflict tax (#661, P3.43)**: entries group under `### YYYY-MM-DD` newest-first *within* the day, so every PR inserts at the same line and **every merge makes every other open PR conflict** — six times across eight PRs on this date alone, always the same file, always the same mechanical resolution. The conflicts themselves are positional, never semantic. The part that is not harmless is that resolving one badly appends a **second** `### <date>` section instead of merging into the first, and nothing notices: that happened during #522 and reached `main`, where it had to be merged back by hand. `scripts/test_changelog_headings.py` fails on any date carrying more than one bare heading, and runs in the existing `pytest scripts/` step of the `Security Audit` job. **Two corrections to my own first attempt, both caught by the guard rather than by re-reading.** The ledger was built from a `grep -oE "^### [0-9]{4}-..."` prefix match while the guard uses an anchored `\s*$` regex, so it listed 13 dates where the guard sees 11 — the extra two are `### 2026-07-21 (evening)` / `(later)` / `(latest)` and `### 2026-07-22 (later)`, which turn out to be an **existing deliberate convention** for splitting a busy day. That makes the anchored form the right one: a suffixed heading is intentional, a bare duplicate is a bad merge. And the first mutation run's revert silently did nothing because the new file was uncommitted, so `git checkout HEAD --` had nothing to restore — the repo's own commit-before-mutating rule, skipped twice in one session after I wrote a live rule about it. Eleven historical duplicates are ledgered rather than merged: reordering historical entries is a large, hard-to-review diff over text whose within-day order may carry meaning, and the value is stopping the next one. Three mutations, each revert asserted: a new duplicate fails, a stale ledger row fails, and breaking the pattern trips an explicit vacuity assertion (`> 40 headings`) rather than passing over an empty match set. **The conflicts themselves are deliberately not addressed** — append-within-day versus one-file-per-entry changes how everyone writes entries, so #661 keeps both options with their trade-offs and leaves the choice to a human.

- **twMerge silently deleted the selected-tab accent, and the dark half was never reported (#640, P3.41)**: `cn()` is `twMerge`, and a generic `border-<color>` collides with a directional `border-r-<color>` — last one wins outright. With the active accent applied *before* the state classes, `ERROR_TAB`'s `border-red-200` deleted `border-r-primary`, so an active errored tab painted a pale strip where the selected-tab affordance should be, on exactly the tab a user is most likely to be in. Reproduced against the real `twMerge` before touching anything, and the same collision confirmed for `UNSAVED_TAB`'s `border-orange-200` and for both orientations. **Two of the issue's figures are wrong and are corrected in the guard**: it quotes the accent at 16.39:1, which is the `--primary` *token* — `border-*` never resolves to it, the identical mistake #634 shipped and corrected — while `border-r-primary` is `tailwind.config.js`'s theme-fixed brand indigo at **6.29:1**; and it transposes its two border figures (`border-red-200` is **1.45**, `border-orange-200` is 1.35). **A second defect, not in the issue at all**: `globals.css` overrides `.dark .text-primary` but **not** `.dark .border-primary`, so the accent stayed brand indigo on dark surfaces — **2.85:1 on `--card`, 2.41 on `--muted`**, under 1.4.11's 3:1. That is the #635 trap a third time. `dark:border-r-indigo-400` takes it to 5.07–6.64, and 4.87–6.21 over the errored dark card. **The naive fix is wrong and the guard pins why**: simply reordering `cn()` so the accent comes first keeps the accent but loses `bg-red-50` to `bg-background`, undoing #634. The accent is split into its own constants and applied *after* the state classes, so the state keeps the other three edges and the card tint while the accent keeps its own edge — verified across all six state/orientation combinations through the real `twMerge`. **Mutation M1 exposed a hole in the first cut of the guard**: moving the accent back above `UNSAVED_TAB` reintroduced the bug and the suite still passed 60/60, because every assertion fed `twMerge` a list with the accent last — true by construction, and true even after the component stops doing it. The guard now reads the component's actual `cn(...)` argument list and asserts the accent's **position** is after both state constants; with that, M1 fails in both placements. Three further mutations: dropping `dark:border-r-indigo-400` fails all three dark 1.4.11 assertions, renaming a constant trips the vacuity count at describe-time, and the state-classes-survive assertion pins the trade the naive fix would have made. 312 theme assertions across 8 suites, 199 ChapterTab tests, and the new #625 `typecheck:tests` gate confirms the guard block adds no type errors.

- **`next dev` was writing an untracked, unignored `frontend/CLAUDE.md` on every start (#611, P3.35)**: Next 16 generates `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md` for AI agents unless told not to, and neither was tracked nor covered by any ignore rule. The `AGENTS.md` half is merely untidy; the `CLAUDE.md` half is the real hazard — **any agent session started in `frontend/` reads it as project instructions**, silently competing with the considered root `CLAUDE.md`, and this repo's workflow uses `git add -A` freely, so a single `npm run dev` followed by a normal stage sweeps in an instruction file nobody chose to add. Took the issue's recommendation — `agentRules: false` in `next.config.ts` (option 2) **with** both paths in `frontend/.gitignore` (option 1) as a second line of defence, so a future Next version that regenerates them regardless still cannot get them staged. **Verified in both directions rather than by absence**: with the flag set, `npm run dev` reaches `✓ Ready` and neither file appears; with the flag removed, the same command logs `✓ Generated AGENTS.md and CLAUDE.md for AI agents. Set 'agentRules: false' in next.config to disable.` and both files land — so it is demonstrably this setting that suppresses them, not a coincidence of environment. The belt-and-braces half was checked separately by creating both files by hand and confirming `git check-ignore` matches and `git add -A -n` stages neither. `npm run typecheck` passing is itself the confirmation that `agentRules` is a real `NextConfig` key rather than an ignored typo. Process note: cleaning up the dev server twice tripped the repo's own `pkill -f self-matches the shell` lesson (exit 144, the invoking shell killed by its own pattern) — the working approach is a captured PID, or `ss -ltnp` to find the listener, never a `-f` pattern that the command line itself contains.

- **The theme-blind toaster was never rendered; the live one was already correct (#638, P3.40)**: the issue reported that "every toast the app raises is a dark card on a light page" because `ui/toaster.tsx` hardcodes `theme="dark"`. **That premise is wrong, and checking it was the whole job.** `app/layout.tsx` imports `ui/sonner.tsx`, not `ui/toaster.tsx`, and the live component already drives `theme` from `useTheme()` and already paints from tokens (`--normal-bg: var(--popover)`, `--normal-text: var(--popover-foreground)`, `--normal-border: var(--border)`). Measured: **19.80:1 light, 17.18:1 dark** — no toast has ever been a dark card on a light page. `ui/toaster.tsx` was a second, unused toaster left over from the nova scaffold, **never imported in any commit** (`git log -S` across all history, the same check #630 used), so it is deleted along with its rows in **both** theme ledgers — 6 gray literals and 4 dark overlays, deleted rather than shrunk, which is what the AC asked for. **Deleting it removed the only thing watching this surface**: those two ledger rows were the sole record that a toaster in this repo had ever been theme-blind, so a new guard replaces them pointed at the toaster that actually renders — `toaster-follows-theme.test.ts` pins that layout still imports `ui/sonner`, that its theme comes from `useTheme()` and never from a literal, that it paints from the three tokens and carries no neutral literal or dark translucent overlay, that each severity has its own icon glyph (WCAG 1.4.1 — colour is not the sole indicator, which is why the four deleted tints lose nothing), and that the popover pair clears 4.5:1 in both themes with the measured figures pinned. Without it, anyone hardcoding `theme="dark"` in `sonner.tsx` tomorrow reproduces #638 exactly with nothing to catch it — the "removing a thing also removes its tripwire" shape from #534 and #630. **Three mutations**: hardcoding `theme="dark"` on the *live* toaster fails the new guard (reproducing the reported defect for real, against the file that actually ships); a `bg-gray-900` literal fails the literal ban; and restoring the deleted file with its ledger rows gone fails **both** the #632 overlay guard and the #620 gray guard, proving those rows were load-bearing rather than decorative. 310 tests across 9 theme suites.

- **`npm run typecheck` covered zero test files; now they are checked against a ledger (#625, P3.38)**: `tsconfig.json` excludes `**/*.test.ts(x)`, `**/__tests__/**`, `jest.setup.ts`, `**/playwright*.ts` and `**/e2e/**`, and jest runs through `next/jest`, whose SWC transpile **strips types without checking them**. Between the two, a test file could pass the wrong argument type, reference a prop that no longer exists, or `await` a non-promise, and every gate stayed green. **Confirmed rather than assumed**, per the repo's own `gate-must-cover-the-file` lesson: `tsc --listFiles` on the base config lists **0** files under `__tests__`. This is not theoretical — it cost two real `TS2345` arity errors in #637's guard earlier the same day, caught only by invoking `tsc` on the file by hand. **The issue said measure before deleting the exclusions, and the measurement chose the design**: removing them outright surfaces **176 errors across 29 files**, which would have blocked the next PR touching any of them. So it takes the ledgered shape the repo already uses for `security-baseline.json` and `gray-literal-baseline.json` — `tsconfig.test.json` (same compiler options, test files included), `typecheck-test-baseline.json` recording per-file counts with a reason naming each file's dominant error code, and `scripts/typecheck-tests.mjs` failing on any file **not** in the ledger or **above** its count, while merely *reporting* rows that have been fixed so nobody's build breaks because someone cleaned up. **All eight theme guards are type-clean and therefore deliberately unledgered** — they are the entire enforcement for #331/#610/#618/#620/#623/#629/#632/#634/#637 and parse CSS and JSON by hand, so a type error in one is a guard that may not be guarding; with no ledger row, any such error fails immediately. Wired into the `Frontend Tests` job, which is branch-protected. **Mutation-checked three ways**, each reverted with the revert asserted: an error injected into `core-authoring-pages-tokens.test.ts` fails as *no ledger row*; one injected into `ChapterTab.keyboard.test.tsx` fails as *above recorded count* (that file was already ledgered at 1, so it exercised the count path rather than the missing-row path — both are the gate working, and worth naming precisely rather than claiming the assertion that did not fire); and one added to `toast.test.ts` trips `6 -> 7`. The script also distinguishes a crashed `tsc` from a clean one — output with no `file(line,col): error TSxxxx` diagnostics is treated as a broken run, not as success, because a gate that reads a failed tool as "no errors" is the false-green this issue is about. Wall clock **6.1s** against the AC's ~30s ceiling (base typecheck is 1.5s); frontend suite unchanged at 144 suites / 2628 passed, lint 0 errors.

- **Deleted `TocSidebar.tsx`, an orphan no module has ever imported (#630, P3.39)**: surfaced by the #623 review, which cross-checked which surfaces `ChapterStatusIndicator`'s dot variant is painted on and found the answer was none. **The issue's caution was the important part** — "a sidebar that was built and then never wired up is a missing feature, not dead code" — so the question was settled from history rather than from a grep: `git log -S` across **all** commits shows it was never imported by any page at any point, and it entered in `ebba5f8` ("Implement tabbed interface for chapters") alongside the tab UI that superseded the sidebar concept. Orphaned by supersession, not by accident. The only two name matches in the tree are a **test-local component in `NavigationFix.test.tsx` that merely shares the name** and never imports the real one. **No ledger row referenced it**, so the stale-row and missing-file assertions in the four baseline guards were unaffected — checked before deleting, because that is precisely the shape that turns a file removal into a red build. **AC3's tripwire already existed and was verified rather than assumed**: the #623 guard reads `components/ui/ChapterStatusIndicator.tsx` *directly* through `DOT_SOURCES`, not through any consumer, so deleting the sole caller of the dot branch does not weaken it. Two mutations prove it: recolouring a status dot to `#f5f5f5` fails four assertions (3:1 on `--background` and `--muted`, both themes), and removing one status colour entry trips the vacuity count at describe-time before a single test runs. The dot branch is now unreachable in the shipped app, which the issue already judged acceptable — the component is public and the branch is one prop away from live — and it stays guarded. One knock-on fixed: #637's guard doc comment cited `TocSidebar.tsx:38` as the nearest thing in the tree to a Prettier-wrapped `cn(...)` splitting a light/dark pair across two string literals; with the file gone the sweep now finds no such shape at all, so the comment says that instead of pointing at a deleted line.

- **Retired the PM2 rollback path, and found two more docs that mislead than the issue listed (#520, P3.29)**: staging cut over to containers on 2026-08-12 (#484) and the PM2/rsync workflows were kept as the rollback route; three weeks and a full container redeploy cycle later they are dead weight. Deleted `deploy-staging.yml.disabled` and `deploy-production.yml.disabled` (git history keeps them recoverable). **Every premise re-verified against the API before acting**, because this issue had already been re-scoped once when its original inventory turned out wrong: both files existed, `DATABASE_NAME` existed only as a `staging` *environment* secret (not repo-level), and the only `secrets.DATABASE_NAME` consumers were the four lines inside the file being deleted — `build-images.yml` and `tests.yml` set it to literal ephemeral values (`smoke`, `auto_author_e2e_test`), which stay. **The docs fix is the part with real cost, and there were two wrong paragraphs, not one.** The issue flagged the rotation runbook's closing advice — "optionally update the `MONGODB_URI` GitHub secret … but note that leaves a second copy of a live credential with no consumer" — which is wrong twice over since #545 gave that secret a live consumer in `sync-staging-env.yml`. Updating it is now **mandatory**, since a stale secret means the next sync run overwrites the box with a dead password. The second wrong paragraph was upstream in the same file and unflagged: §1 asserted the `MONGODB_URI` secret "is referenced only by `deploy-staging.yml.disabled` … Changing it does not affect what staging connects with." Following either statement during a rotation is what #537's outage cost. **A third stale doc found while checking cross-references**: `docs/CI_CD_RECOVERY_PLAN.md` still headed itself `**Status:** CRITICAL - Staging Deployments Blocked`, dated 2025-10-24, with no historical marker, describing the very deploy path being deleted — an operator finding it today reads a resolved incident as a live crisis. Marked historical with a pointer to the current deployment docs (`deployment-pipeline-architecture.md` already carried such a marker and needed nothing). **One acceptance criterion is not done and is not mine to do**: deleting the `DATABASE_NAME` staging environment secret is an irreversible out-of-repo action and the `gh api -X DELETE` call was correctly refused by the permission gate. Nothing reads it any more and no information is lost by deleting it — the value `auto_author_staging` is already in the repo in plaintext and the operative copy lives in `/opt/auto-author/.env`, which is what compose reads — so it is handed over with the exact command rather than left implied.

- **The backend lint hook was vacuous three ways, and turning it on found a real bug (#561, P3.28)**: `entry: bash -c 'cd backend && uv run ruff check app/ tests/ || true'` could not fail for three independent reasons — `|| true` swallowed the exit code, `ruff` was in neither the main deps nor the `test` extra so `uv run ruff` died with `Failed to spawn`, and that failure was swallowed too.
